  **Since #31, `search_sites` and `similar_sites` read a site's other names** (`alt_names`) — folded by one owner, `folded_names`, and indexed by a trigram index of their own (`dive_sites_alt_names_fold_trgm_idx`) — and `similar_sites` takes the new site's (`p_alt_names`), so §2.3's check runs both ways. `suggest_site_edit` lets a suggestion propose every fact #31 gave a site, which #54's edit and suggestion pages build on.
  **Both gaps M2c found here are closed** (M2j): `search_centers` exists, and `unaccent` gives the folding its server half. **One remains and is deliberate: there is no `similar_centers`.** §5 asks for the fuzzy duplicate check about a *site*, so a centre twin would be inventing a requirement rather than filling a hole — about forty lines the day this paragraph asks for it.

**Free-tier caveat:** free projects pause after 7 idle days. A weekly GitHub Action pinging a health endpoint keeps it warm during quiet months; once there are real users, the problem disappears — and the app works offline regardless.

**Admin in v1 is Supabase Studio.** The merge and suggestion queues are just tables to review there, and usage stats (users, dives per week, new sites) are SQL views. A real `/admin` area arrives with the web app and reuses its codebase.

## 6. Data model

The same schema lives in SQLite (Drizzle) and Postgres. Conventions: **SI units stored**, converted at display · IDs are **client-generated UUIDv7**, so offline creation never needs re-mapping · every column nullable except `id`, `user_id`, `date` · all synced tables carry `created_at`, `updated_at` and `deleted_at` (tombstone). `created_at` is not bookkeeping: dive numbering uses it as an ordering tier for same-day dives with neither a time nor a hand-set order (§2.5), so it must be preserved across sync rather than regenerated.

**On `updated_at`:** the client stamps it locally on every write, and §7's `push_changes` restamps it with the server clock from M2 onwards. Until M2 exists there is no server to set it, so an M1 reader should not go hunting for server code — the repository is the only writer. It is load-bearing either way: §7's whole-row last-write-wins is keyed on this column, so a write that changes nothing must not advance it, or the device that did nothing wins the conflict against the device that did something.

**On `tanks` being non-nullable** (the one exception to "everything nullable"): an empty array already means "no cylinders recorded", so a nullable column would add a second way to say the same thing and force every reader to handle both. The column is NOT NULL with a `'[]'` default and the domain type is `Tank[]`, not `Tank[] | null`.

### `dives` — private, one row per dive

| Cluster | Fields |
|---|---|
| Identity | `status` (logged·planned) · `date` · `time_in` · `manual_order` · `duration_min` · `title` · `notes` · `rating` (1–5) |
| Where | `site_id` + `site_name` snapshot · `center_id` + `center_name` snapshot · `entry` (shore·boat·other) · `salinity` (salt·fresh) · `water_body` (ocean·lake·river·quarry·cave·pool) · `latitude` + `longitude` (optional exact GPS point) |
| Profile & conditions | `max_depth_m` · `avg_depth_m` · `water_temp_c` · `air_temp_c` · `visibility` (high·average·low) + `visibility_m` · `waves` (0–3) · `current` (0–3) · `surge` (0–3) · `weather` (sunny·partly·cloudy·rainy·windy·foggy) |
| Gas & cylinders | `tanks` — JSON array, one entry per cylinder, first = main: `{ material (steel·alu), configuration (single·twinset·sidemount), sizeL, workingBar, o2Pct (21 = air), hePct, startBar, endBar }`. **Nitrogen is never stored** — it is 100 − O₂ − He and is drawn as a computed value (§0.6). |
| Equipment & people | `suit` (none·shorty·wet·semidry·dry) · `suit_thickness_mm` · `equipment` — token set (hood·gloves·boots·torch·camera) · `weights_kg` + `weights_feel` (under·good·over) · `buddy` · `guide` |

**What a column becomes in Postgres** (M2a, because "the same schema lives in both" did not say). **Calendar dates and clock times are `text`, not `date`/`time`** — `storedCalendarDate` deliberately stores an uninterpretable date unchanged (§1 never blocks a save), and §7 pushes in one transaction, so a `date` column would freeze a diver's entire sync over one bad row. **Timestamps are `timestamptz`**, which §7's ordering depends on. **No vocabulary column is an enum and none carries a CHECK** — §10 already rules that a value from a client this build does not know is stored and flagged rather than rejected, and an enum makes that impossible without a migration; this is that ruling arriving in the section a schema author actually reads.

**On the GPS point:** SQLite has no point type, so a dive's optional exact position is two nullable columns on the device. Postgres composes them into a PostGIS point — the sync payload carries the pair, and the server owns the geometry. **Not built in M2a and deliberately so:** nothing in v1 reads that geometry, and a generated column wrapping PostGIS is the single statement most likely to fail on a server nobody here can test against. The `alter table` is written down in `supabase/README.md`. If it is still undone when M2 closes it belongs on §9's shelf rather than sitting in this paragraph reading as done. `dive_sites` keeps a single PostGIS `location` because that table is server-authoritative.

**A dive's site is two columns and they are one fact.** `site_id` and `site_name` are kept together or re-synced together, **never one of each** — a dive holding one site's spelling against another's id is a row that no screen can report, because the name reads correctly and the id points at something the diver never saw. The form protects this in its seed rather than with a dirty flag, and §10 records why a flag cannot: a write whose value equals what the form is about to reset to is *pristine*, records nothing, and is therefore silently re-synced — which is exactly what happens to the half that matches.

**The keys inside `tanks` are camelCase, unlike every column name.** That is deliberate. Column names get a mapping layer — camelCase in TypeScript, snake_case in SQL — but a JSON blob's interior gets none: whatever the app writes is what is stored, and changing it later means rewriting every row *and* re-agreeing with Postgres `jsonb` in M2. camelCase is the lower-friction end, since the app reads these keys directly.

Tanks are one JSON column instead of a child table: they are never queried on their own, and whole-row sync stays trivial. The form shows a single cylinder until "+ add cylinder" is tapped — multi-gas ready without multi-gas clutter.

**Computed in the app, never stored:** the dive number (chronology + `dives_before` offset), used pressure, RMV across all tanks, MOD when diving nitrox, time out, surface interval.

### Other tables

- **`dive_samples`** (private, real from M3o — reserved in prose here from the start, and the importer is what finally created it): one column of content, `series`, holding a JSON array of `[seconds from the dive's start, depth m, °C or null, bar or null]` tuples, one entry per sample. The last two are nullable because a dive logged without a tank transmitter carries no pressure at all (§10) and a sample need not carry a temperature. Roughly 12 bytes a point — about 2.7 KB for the 222-point reference dive.
  **`id` IS the dive's own id**, and that is the whole shape of the table: there is exactly one series per dive, so this is a **1:1 extension of the dive rather than a child collection**. Sharing the id makes *"does this dive have a profile"* a primary-key lookup, makes a re-import a plain upsert, lets a dive's deletion tombstone its series without a query, and needs neither a join key nor a unique index. It is still a client-generated UUIDv7 — just the dive's, not a fresh one.
  **Not many rows, and not a column on `dives`.** The first half is the ruling §10 already made about `tanks`, for the same reason: a profile is never queried independently, since one dive's series is read to draw one dive's chart. The second half is why it is not simply another column on the dive: every screen in the app reads dives and almost none reads profiles, so a `series` column would make the app's most-travelled query carry ~3 KB of profile per row for a chart that is not on screen.
  **Time is seconds from the dive's start, computed from each sample's own timestamp** — never index × the declared sample interval. The reference file declares 10 s and its real deltas run 9.74–10.10 s, so an index-derived series would drift against the timestamps it claims to represent. **`series` is `jsonb` in Postgres**, like `tanks` and `equipment`; §10 records that being specified as `text` and what that would have cost.
- **`dive_sites`** (community): `name` · `country` · `location` (PostGIS point) · `salinity` · `water_body` · `entry` — token set (shore·boat·other): every way in the site offers, where a dive's `entry` is the one way it used; a set under its singular name since #31 (§10) · `max_depth_m` (site depth — typed on the create page since #31, never inherited from a dive, §2.3) · `altitude_m` (metres above sea level, negative below it, whole metres as saved; a site's own, not a depth — typed on the create page, or taken from the GPS fix's offer there with a tap, #31) · `site_types` — token set (wreck·reef·wall·pinnacle·canyon·swim_through·muck·drift·blue_water; several may hold, a wreck on a wall, and a cave is already `water_body = cave`) · `suitable_for` (open_water·advanced·experienced·technical) · `alt_names` — JSON array of text, the other names the place goes by (*Stone Gate* for *Kamena vrata*), which search and the duplicate check read (§2.3) · `description` · `wreck_name` · `wreck_sunk_year` (integer; the two asked only while `site_types` holds `wreck`) · `created_by` · `status` (active·merged·hidden) · `merged_into`. A site's defaults prefill new dives logged there.
  **`suitable_for` is typed, not computed**: a shallow site with a strong current is still *advanced*, which no depth figure can say. Its levels are named by what a diver can handle rather than by one agency's course — *open_water* shallow, to about 18 m, and easy; *advanced* to about 30 m, or with current, drift, low visibility or a tricky entry; *experienced* to about 40 m, or with strong current and demanding conditions; *technical* beyond recreational limits, decompression or overhead.
- **`dive_centers`** (community): `name` · `country` · `location` · `website` · `address` (one free-text line — *Otok Vis, Kut 12* — never split into street, city and postcode, because addresses do not share one shape around the world and its job is to get a person to the shop) · `phone` · `email` · `opening_hours` (each free text, as the centre writes it) · `services` — token set (air·nitrox·trimix·rental·courses·boat·shore·repair) · `agencies` — token set (padi·ssi·cmas·naui·sdi_tdi·raid·gue·bsac·iantd·other — closed, with *other*, where the wallet's `agency` is free text; §10 says why the two differ) · `description` · `created_by` · `status` · `merged_into`. The last was missing from this list while §5 described merging "a site or center" in one breath — a `status` that can read `merged` with nowhere to point is a state with no repair (M2a).
  **What issue #31 added, as a rule** (2026-11-17): every new column is nullable except the sets — `services` and `agencies` here, `site_types` and `alt_names` above, and a site's `entry`, which became one in place — which are NOT NULL with a `'[]'` default exactly as `dives.equipment` is (`jsonb` in Postgres), so "none recorded" has one spelling. No vocabulary column is an enum or carries a CHECK (above). **A `description` is public**, on a site as on a centre: multi-line, read by every diver, and labelled so on the page that asks for it (§0.6) so nobody writes a private note there. The figures a diver's own logs can compute — water temperature by month, visibility, the depths actually dived, how many divers have been — have no column, and stay with #43.
- **`gear_presets`** (private): `name` + the cylinders/gas block (the `tanks` array, minus the pressures). The table keeps its name — §7 pushes it by that name and renaming it buys a better word at the cost of a migration and a sync-protocol edit — but it no longer carries `suit`, `hood`, `gloves`, `boots` or `weights_kg`; see §10.
- **`site_duplicate_suspicions`** (community, server-only, added M2q): the ordered pair `site_id` + `candidate_id` as its primary key, plus `status`. **A suspicion is about a pair and one site may be suspected of duplicating several**, which is why it is a table rather than a column — a column keeps the last suspicion and loses the rest. The three states §5's flow needs are the **row itself**: absent is *never suspected*, present is `open`, and a diver's answer is `dismissed` or `merged`. That makes `on conflict … do nothing` the whole of "a rerun never resurrects a dismissal", where a nullable column on `dive_sites` would have needed a second column free to disagree with it. Keeping it off `dive_sites` earns two more things: `pull_changes` renders whole rows, so a column there would broadcast every suspicion to every device; and nothing here touches `dive_sites.updated_at`, so §7's last-write-wins gets no server echo for the client to pull back.
- **`site_edits`** (community, added M2c because §5 required a review queue this list never named): the suggested `dive_site_id` · the proposing `created_by` · the fields being proposed · a free-text `note` · `status`. Server-only — it has no SQLite counterpart, since a suggestion is made online and reviewed in Studio. A departed diver's `note` is **kept** with authorship severed rather than cleared: keeping is the reversible direction, and the text is usually the part an admin needs ("the pin is on the far side of the jetty").
- **`certifications`** (private): `agency` · `course` · `card_number` · `issued_on` · `expires_on` (O₂, first aid). Manual entry — agencies expose no public API. Card photos join with v1.1 photos.
  **`agency` is free text, and §10's store-and-flag ruling is the argument *against* a vocabulary here rather than for one** (M3b). That ruling's point is that a value this build does not know is kept and flagged — but its note blames a newer client, and that sentence is false for a field the diver typed themselves. A closed list would tell a BSAC or a CMAS-federation diver that their own card is unrecognised. §2.3's suggest-from-history was considered and rejected on scale: a wallet holds about five cards in a lifetime, so there is no history to learn from.
  **An expiry is stated as a fact and never as a nudge** — `expired 3 Sep 2026` in the row, no hue (§0.1), no icon. §3 gives *currency* and the refresher sentence to the Stats screen, which is where a prompt belongs. A card expiring **today** is current, and the comparison is between calendar dates rather than instants: the naive version calls an expired card good for a whole local morning east of UTC.
- **`profiles`**: `display_name` · `dives_before` · (future `is_supporter`).
- **Local only:** `settings` (units · locale · the form-group memory · `dives_before`, which syncs to the profile) · `sync_state` (`last_pulled_at` — the server's, never the phone's).
  **Two corrections this line carried until M2d.** It said *hidden groups*, which was "Fields I use" — dropped in M1i, and §2.2's collapse rule is what replaced it. And it put the **dirty flags inside `sync_state`**, which contradicts §7's own wording: a flag is **per row**, a column on each synced table, because "which rows are owed" is a fact about rows and a single state row cannot hold it. `sync_state` holds one timestamp and nothing else.
- **The catalogue lives on the device too**, which this section never said while §5 and §2.3 both required it (M2d). `dive_sites` and `dive_centers` exist in SQLite column-for-column with Postgres, the one difference being that `location` becomes the `latitude`/`longitude` pair — §6's own rule for a dive's pin, applied a second time. Without them `pull_changes` returns a catalogue with nowhere to put it and §2.3's *"the on-device copy… fully offline"* is a promise nothing keeps. **They have two writers with different rules and therefore different types**: a site created on a boat is dirty and will be pushed; a row arriving from a pull is not, and the function that applies it takes a type with nowhere to say otherwise.

**Reserved now** so nothing migrates painfully later: `dive_photos` (storage paths per dive). Until `dive_samples` holds rows for a dive, no curve is drawn for it anywhere — see §0.4, which is still true and now for the first time has a way of becoming false.

**Two of the three reservations were spent in M3o, and they had not been made the same way.** `import_source` and `import_id` were reserved as **real columns**, and the importer used them exactly as written — no migration, no widening, and the dedupe rule §10 states needed nothing added. `dive_samples` was reserved *in prose*, which reserved the name and none of the design: everything above had to be decided from scratch the moment something needed the table. Worth keeping in mind the next time this section reserves something.

## 7. Sync protocol

A deliberately small, WatermelonDB-style delta sync — two RPCs, whole-row overwrite. Safe here because every private row has exactly one author; conflicts only occur between one person's own devices.

**The server's rule is last *push* wins, not last *write*, and the difference is worth stating** (M2g, where this paragraph said "last-write-wins" and the SQL never compared anything). `push_changes` upserts with no `where` clause and stamps `updated_at` with the moment the row arrived, so a phone that has been offline for a week goes up carrying older content and overwrites what the server holds. **Kept deliberately.** Genuine last-write-wins would mean comparing a timestamp the *phone* wrote, and §7.3 exists precisely because phone clocks cannot be trusted — a device an hour fast would win every conflict for as long as it stayed wrong. And the loser here is always the *other* device's edit, never the pushing device's own, which is the direction that matters: the diver doing the pushing is the one looking at the screen. Changing it costs a comparison in seven upserts **and** a client-side answer for a row the server declined, which is a design question rather than a clause. (Six until M3o added `dive_samples`; the number is written out here because it is the cost of reversing the decision, so it grows every time a table joins.)

**Six tables go through this, not five** (M3o). `dive_samples` joined `dives`, `gear_presets`, `certifications`, `dive_sites` and `dive_centers` as **one more `synced({…})` entry and no protocol change at all** — which is the test the seam was built to pass, and what made adding it safe is §4.1's parity guard: a column added on one side and not the other fails `schemaParity` rather than syncing to nowhere. **A series is immutable**, so last-write-wins has nothing to adjudicate here beyond picking the newer row — nothing in the app edits a profile, and re-importing the same file produces byte-identical content. That makes LWW trivially correct for this table in a way it is not for a dive. **The one number that moves is the payload**: a profile is ~3 KB where §9's web spike sized a dive at ~600 bytes, so the 1 MiB ceiling that spike found sits at a few hundred profiles rather than the ~1,700 dives it was quoted for, and this is the table that reaches it first. Server cost is unaffected — 1.5 MB for a 500-dive logbook, which does not move §8's line. Native `expo-sqlite` has no such limit either; the ceiling is `wa-sqlite`'s bridge buffer, on the platform §9 calls a testing target rather than a supported one.

1. **Push.** Rows flagged dirty go up in one transactional `push_changes` call — dives, presets, certifications, a dive's depth profile, and any sites or centers created offline. RLS validates ownership; the server stamps `updated_at` and returns canonical rows; the client clears its flags.

   **A site arriving here is fuzzy-checked again** (§5's promise, built in M2q). The ids are collected **before** the upsert, because afterwards the answer is "nothing new" for ever; the check asks `similar_sites` rather than re-implementing what a duplicate is (§4.1) — handing it the site's other names too since #31, so the recheck asks §2.3's question both ways, as the check before saving does; and it **cannot block the push** — its one exception handler wraps the recheck alone and warns to the server log, because a site that looks like a duplicate is still that diver's site and §7's push is how a device clears its dirty flags at all. **Only the server can tell an arrival from a retry**, which is why this lives here rather than in the client: a device cannot know whether the row it is pushing is new to everyone else, and a client-side recheck fails silently on exactly the offline case §5 wrote this for.
2. **Pull.** `pull_changes(last_pulled_at)` returns the user's changed rows plus the compact community catalogue — all of it while the community is small, country-scoped later. The client upserts by comparing `updated_at`, **and only over a row it does not still owe the server** (M2g). That second condition is not a refinement, it is what stops one device losing an edit to itself: push restamps `updated_at` with the server's clock, so the server's echo of a row can outrank an edit made on the phone *after* the push went out, purely because phones run behind. Under a timestamp alone the echo wins and takes the dirty flag with it — the very flag that would have sent the edit. A dirty row is therefore left alone until it has gone up, and the server settles it on the next push. **The consequence, stated rather than left emergent: a row that cannot be pushed stops receiving server updates entirely, for as long as that lasts.**

   **The last step of a pull is following any merge it delivered** — the catalogue arrives first, then every dive pointing at a row that has just become `merged` is repointed (§5). It is the last step because it reads what the pull has already written (M2r).

   **And a deleted dive's live profile is deleted with it** (#9, `tombstoneSamplesOfDeletedDives`). Deleting a dive tombstones its profile on the device that deletes, so an ordinary remote delete arrives as two tombstones. A profile can still be live beside a dive this device holds as deleted: one written here after the dive was deleted elsewhere (the import's completion of a profile that failed to save, §10), or one an older build left. After the whole pull is applied, such a profile is tombstoned as this device's own write, stamped and dirty, so the server's live copy is deleted on the next push. It runs after the whole change set for the merge's reason: `dives` is applied before `dive_samples`, and tombstoning in between would overwrite the tombstone the same pull was about to deliver with a needless push. It runs on every pull, writes nothing when nothing matches, and leaves alone a profile whose dive is not on this device.

   **A tombstone is applied, not deleted.** This step used to read "tombstoned rows are removed locally", which is not what the client does or should do: the tombstone is *written* to the local row and `liveRows` (§4.1) hides it from every read. Deleting it would throw away the fact of the deletion, which is the one thing a tombstone exists to carry.
   **The comparison is a string comparison, so the RPCs must return timestamps in the client's own `toISOString()` spelling** (M2a). Postgres will happily hand back `2026-09-02 09:00:00+00`, which sorts differently from `2026-09-02T09:00:00.000Z` — and the failure is not an error, it is **the wrong device winning a conflict**, silently, on rows nobody is watching.
   **The watermark a pull returns is deliberately behind the clock** (M2b). `now()` in Postgres is transaction start, so a push committing after the pull took its snapshot carries an earlier stamp than the watermark — and would be skipped **on every future pull, permanently**. The response therefore returns `now() - 1 minute`, so the next pull re-reads a small overlap; re-reading is free because the upsert is idempotent. There is no exact answer computable from inside the function, and a lost row here is silent.
3. **Server clock only.** `last_pulled_at` comes from the server's response, never the phone's clock — divers change time zones constantly.
4. **Guest → account.** On first sign-in, every local row is marked dirty and pushed; the server writes the owner. That is the entire "optional account" migration — the payoff of client-side UUIDs. **This used to read "local rows get the new `user_id`", which described a column that does not exist** (M2b): the device has no `user_id` anywhere, because a device holds exactly one person's logbook and a column repeating the same value on every row would be storage pretending to be a key. Nothing on the client changes at sign-in but the dirty flags.

   **There are three destructive acts, and clearing one device is not among them** (owner's call). A local-only clear that keeps the account is **a no-op with extra steps**: the next pull brings everything back, and the one case it seemed to serve — handing the phone on — is what sign-out already does. So:

   | | |
   |---|---|
   | **Sign out** | wipes this device, the account keeps everything |
   | **Start over** | wipes this device **and the account's data**, and the account survives |
   | **Delete account** | wipes both and the account with them (§8's App Store requirement) |

   **Start over must go through tombstones, not a server-side purge**, and this is the part that decides the implementation. A hard delete on the server leaves a second device holding rows it believes are unsynced — and its next push **puts the whole logbook back**. A tombstone is the only form of deletion §7 can carry to a device that was not there when it happened. **Community rows are not included** in either start-over or deletion: a site or centre the diver contributed belongs to everyone who has dived it, and §5 has never allowed hard-deleting one.

   **The order is tombstone → push → count → erase, and the last three are sign-out's own steps** (M3j): a tombstone is an ordinary dirty row, so the gate that already refuses to wipe what the server has not acknowledged counts them for free. **When it refuses, the tombstones stay** — written, flagged and hidden — and only the erase is deferred, because the tombstones *are* the deletion and dropping them would undo it. That needs its own sentence to the diver: "nothing was cleared" is a lie once their logbook is already empty on screen.

   **Deleting the account has no such gate, and adding one would be wrong rather than merely useless** (M3j). After the RPC the rows belong to a `user_id` that no longer exists, so the push fails, the count comes back non-zero, and a gate would **refuse the erase — leaving a full logbook on a phone whose account is gone.** The gate protects data the server might still want; there is no server left to want it.

   **Signing out wipes the local logbook** (owner's call, M2b). The data is on the server, signing back in re-syncs it, and a device that keeps one person's dives after they have left is the only way a second account could ever see them. So sign-out is a real erase, and the app must say so before it happens — it is the one destructive action in v1.

   **The wipe restores the device to its guest state: everything that came from an account goes, everything the diver set on this device stays.** `dives` and `gear_presets` go. `sync_state` goes, and that one is not optional — a stale `last_pulled_at` makes the next account's first pull skip every row older than it, silently and with no repair. The catalogue tables go too, which is the least obvious of the four: they arrive by pull, so a guest never had them — beyond the places a guest creates, which since #19 are the only catalogue rows a guest holds (§5) — and keeping them would leave a site created offline sitting in the next account's dirty set to be pushed as **their** creation. `settings` **stays** — units, locale and the form-group memory are things this diver set on this device and re-asking would be hostile — with `dives_before` the one exception, because §6 syncs it to the profile and leaving it would hand the next account a wrong pre-Ponor number that shifts every dive number after it.

   **The erase must visit the rows it deletes** (M2i, found by the owner on the first real sign-out). A `DELETE FROM t` with no `WHERE` is not a small delete — SQLite's truncate optimisation clears the table's pages without walking them, and therefore never fires the update hook that `expo-sqlite`'s change listener, and so every live read on screen, is driven by. The device was correctly emptied and the list went on drawing three dives that no longer existed, header summary included, until the app was restarted. **On a device that has just been signed out, that is the app appearing to hold a logbook that has left it** — this section's own purpose, inverted by a display. Every wipe therefore carries a condition true of every row, which costs a page walk and buys the notification.

   **Sign-out pushes first, and refuses if anything is still dirty** (M2g, discharging M2e's build-order constraint by replacing it with the rule it was standing in for). The confirmation promises the logbook comes back, which is true only of rows the server has. So the wipe attempts a push and then **counts what is still owed** — and it counts the rows themselves rather than believing the push's own report, because a push that returns success while storing nothing is exactly the failure this guard exists for. Nothing is deleted before that count is taken. This covers what the ordering constraint never could: the diver who has been offline a month, the push that half-succeeded, the build with no backend at all.

   **Local dives are always adopted, even by an account that already has dives** (M2b). The case is a diver who logs a few offline and then signs into an existing logbook, and the question is only interesting because the phone might have been someone else's. Adopting is right anyway, on an asymmetry: adopting wrongly puts a handful of visible, deletable dives in the wrong logbook, and discarding wrongly destroys dives that exist nowhere else. **UUIDs mean there is nothing to reconcile** — the local rows push as new rows, the account's rows arrive in the pull, and nothing collides. The app **states what it did** afterwards rather than asking first: a prompt at sign-in is a wall in front of the one flow §1 promises is optional, and the answer it collects is one the diver can give better by looking at the list.
5. **Triggers.** **A session arriving**, app foreground, a debounced 10 s after any save, and pull-to-refresh — with a retry ladder standing in for "connectivity restored".

   **A session arriving is the trigger this list was missing** (M2h), and without it the other four do not close the case §7.4 opens: `AppState` is already `active` at launch, so there is no foreground *transition* to fire on, and a diver who signs back in after the sign-out erase would sit looking at an empty logbook whose rows are safe on the server and which nothing on the device would ever ask for. It is keyed on the **user id** rather than on the session object, because a token refresh hands over a fresh session every hour.

   **"Connectivity restored" is not observed.** `@react-native-community/netinfo` is a native dependency and a rebuild; instead a failed cycle retries on a doubling delay from 30 s to a five-minute ceiling. That covers the case that matters — the phone regains signal while the diver is looking at their list and saving nothing — without claiming to know anything about the radio. It is later than netinfo would be, and it cannot tell a lost signal from a server refusing this device.

   **The save window does not restart.** A trailing-edge debounce is pushed forward by every save inside it, so a diver saving every eight seconds would never sync at all. The first save arms the window and every save inside it rides that cycle: eight edits, one sync, never more than ten seconds behind the first of them.

   **One cycle at a time, never while signed out, never on a build with no backend.** Two overlapping cycles are not two syncs — both push the same rows and each clears flags against a clock the other has had restamped, so a flag gets cleared for a row the server took a different version of. The refusals live in the engine rather than at the trigger sites, and the session is re-read at the moment a cycle runs: a save window is ten seconds long and signing out is one tap. **The wipe takes the same lock**, because triggers created a window in which a cycle lands between the erase and the sign-out and pulls the logbook back onto a device that is leaving the account.

   **A quiet indicator shows pending changes; sync failures never block logging.** It is the Dives header's third line — `3 changes waiting to sync`, in the summary's own muted mono — and it appears **only when signed in**, because every write flags a row whether or not there is an account and a guest would otherwise read "128 changes waiting to sync" with nowhere to send them. It is not a fourth segment on the summary line: those three figures describe one population, and this describes the device's obligation. **An automatic failure says nothing** — the line simply stays, which is the true report — and only pull-to-refresh, which a diver asked for, is answered in words.

## 8. Costs, compliance & risks

| Item | Cost | Covers |
|---|---|---|
| Supabase Free | $0 / mo | 500 MB database, 1 GB storage, 50k monthly auth users |
| Expo EAS Free | $0 / mo | Cloud-build quota, plus unlimited local builds |
| Maps, Sentry, GitHub | $0 / mo | Mobile map SDKs are free; free tiers cover hobby scale |
| Apple Developer Program | $99 / yr | Unavoidable for the App Store |
| Google Play | $25 once | Unavoidable for the Play Store |

The first real bill would be Supabase Pro ($25/mo) when the database nears 500 MB or point-in-time backups become worth having — thousands of active users away. Dive rows are tiny; photos in v1.1 are the thing to watch, hence on-device compression and per-user caps when they land.

**If it ever needs to pay for itself:** a tip jar plus an optional supporter subscription that raises photo-storage caps — the logbook itself stays free forever, and no ads. The groundwork today is a single `is_supporter` flag on the profile; RevenueCat's free tier can handle store billing when that day comes.

### Compliance & privacy

- **In-app account deletion** via `delete_account`, a hard App Store requirement, and Sign in with Apple, which becomes one the day Google sign-in is offered (#13, after v1).
- **The personal data is an email address, a display name, and whatever a diver chooses to type.** This line read *"PII is an email address, nothing more"* until M3b, and it had been wrong since M2a: `certifications.card_number` is a real identifier on a real card, `profiles.display_name` is a name, and `site_edits.note` is free text an identified diver wrote. None of it is sensitive-category data and all of it is the diver's own row under RLS — but the old sentence was a claim about the whole system, the kind that gets quoted into a store listing or a privacy policy, and it stopped being true the moment a table landed rather than when anyone decided anything. **A field a diver types is personal data by default; the question is only how much.**
- Data in the EU; RLS on every table; tokens in the device keychain; full data export any time — CSV for spreadsheets, JSON for portability (GDPR Art. 20).
  **The two files answer different questions and are built differently** (M3i). **JSON is the faithful one**: SI, every column, nothing derived, no BOM — the file that could rebuild the logbook. **CSV is for a spreadsheet**, so it follows the diver's own units and **names the unit in every header** (`max_depth_ft`, `water_temp_c`), which takes both halves of a trade the JSON beside it makes safe to take. **Figures keep a decimal point in Czech too**: a comma inside a number in a comma-separated file is the ambiguity quoting exists to prevent, and a Czech archive must not be a different format from an English one. Both carry only the **diver's own** rows — the community catalogue is everybody's, and a copy of it is not their data under Art. 20.
- **Places divers add are published under the ODbL** (the owner's call, issue #31, 2026-11-17), and the terms of use will say so — #15 carries it into the published terms. It is OpenStreetMap's own licence, so an import from OpenStreetMap stays possible without asking every contributor (§5), and anyone who reuses the catalogue must share theirs back.
- **Free text in the catalogue is public, so moderation (#57) blocks the public release (#22).** Since #31 a site and a centre carry a description, a site its other names and a wreck's name, and a centre an address, a phone number, an email and opening hours, each typed by one diver and shown to every other. A store release with no way to report or hide what one diver wrote about a place would take on the moderation duties §9 holds social features back from until the app has earned them.

### Risks

- **Sync bugs are the classic trap.** Mitigated by the smallest viable protocol: whole-row LWW, server clock, client UUIDs, tombstones — and integration tests that simulate two devices from day one.
- **Community data gets messy.** Fuzzy dedupe at creation, name snapshots on dives, and admin merge via `status`/`merged_into`. Moderation tooling stays Supabase Studio until the app earns real traffic.
- **Scope creep.** v1 is the **v1** GitHub milestone, and #22 says why each issue is in it (2026-11-19, §9 and §10): whatever changes the shape of stored data, plus the core logging flow, goes in before release, because after it strangers' dives are real data and older builds keep syncing the old shape. Everything else — photos, the import formats M3o did not build, the web app, social features — goes on the shelf after release, not into v1.

## 9. Milestones

**Open work is tracked as GitHub issues** (since 2026-11-14). This section keeps each milestone's record, its done-when and the post-release shelf; the open items below carry their issue number, and a line here is updated with the outcome when its issue closes.

- **M0 · Skeleton** — repo, Expo + TypeScript app, dev builds running in a simulator or on a device, lint and CI green. Plus the §0 identity in code: both token sets driving a `StyleSheet` theme, the depth scale, Archivo + IBM Plex Mono loaded, app icon and splash. A weekend.
  *Done when: the app runs from CI-checked code; **the four Archivo weights render visibly different from each other and the IBM Plex Mono rows render monospaced**; and switching the OS between light and dark switches the app with it. **Met 2026-08-29** on the iOS 26.3 simulator. A physical-device pass is worth doing before release but is not an M0 gate.*

  > The typeface criterion is worded that way deliberately. It first read "Czech diacritics intact", which San Francisco renders perfectly — so the check passed while **no embedded font resolved on iOS at all**, and M0 was briefly marked met on evidence that could not fail. Weight difference and monospacing are falsifiable; diacritics are not. Apply the same test to future done-whens: ask what result would *disprove* the claim.
- **M1 · The local logbook** — schema and migrations, the dive form with groups + prefill + cylinder presets, prepare-ahead planned dives, list and detail, units, autocomplete from own history. **Duplicate-a-dive was cut** (owner's call, M1e — §10). No account, fully offline — already a usable app.
  *Done when: you log a 3-dive trip in under 3 minutes, in airplane mode. **Met 2026-09-01** — 129 s on an iPhone 17 Pro, three dives at four interactions each (`+` · max depth · duration · Save), site and centre carried every time.*

  > Two honesty notes on that number, in the spirit of M0's own. **The airplane-mode half was satisfied by killing Metro outright rather than flipping the toggle** — a dev client streams its JS from Metro, so the toggle breaks the harness rather than testing the app; the app went on listing and writing from the loaded bundle and on-device SQLite with nothing behind it. It is reinforced by a static fact rather than a demonstration: there is no network call anywhere in `src/` and no networked dependency installed, so there is nothing in M1 a radio could fail. **And the run was agent-driven at fixed coordinates, so it measures the flow's length, not a diver's speed** — four interactions per dive is the real finding. Worth re-running by hand once, on a real device.
- **M2 · Accounts & community** — Supabase project, **email sign-in**, the sync protocol, community sites and centers with fuzzy dedupe, and the Map tab.
  *Done when: a site created offline on "the boat" appears on a second signed-in device's map after sync, and an obvious duplicate gets caught by "did you mean".*
  **The duplicate half is met; the sync half has never been run** (owner's call to defer it). It needs two signed-in devices and nobody working in this repository has credentials, so it is the one acceptance test here that cannot be self-served. Every piece under it is tested and **the seam between them is not** — which is precisely where this milestone's real defects have been found. Deferred, not met.
  **Apple and Google moved to M3** (owner's call). This line asked for three sign-in methods; one shipped. They are release requirements rather than functional gaps — email works, and the Apple Developer account they need is the one the store submission needs anyway.
- **M3 · Surface — release** — stats screen, certification wallet, Czech + English, CSV + JSON export, **Sign in with Apple and Google** (§5: Apple is mandatory once Google is offered), store listings, Sentry wired, **branded auth email**, TestFlight and Play internal testing, then public. **Dive import was added** (owner's call, 2026-09-08, pulled forward out of v1.1 — below, and §10). **The release's scope was settled on 2026-11-19** (#22, at the end of this milestone): Apple, Google and Play moved after it, and the logging and data-shape work and an OpenStreetMap import moved into it.

  **Built:** the Stats tab · the certification wallet · Czech and English throughout · CSV and JSON export · the three destructive acts (sign out, start over, delete account — §8's App Store requirement) · **dive import, reading Suunto's JSON** (M3o). **Also landed in this stretch though not in the line above**, because using the app kept finding them: dive centres had no surface at all, the map's layers became a filter with three marks, dive sites had no detail page, and RMV gained a sparkline.

  **Three exits that lost work, all found by using the form** (M3m). Each corrects reasoning that was already written down here, which is why they are one note rather than three bug lines.

  **The save control sat behind the keyboard.** §0.5 puts the primary action in the bottom third, and every number on this form takes a `decimal-pad` — which on iOS has **no return key at all** — so with the keyboard up *Save dive* was not merely low, it was covered, and there was no key on that keyboard to put it away with. The diver's only exit was to find a blank strip of form to tap. `formFooter` was already an in-flow sibling below the scroll rather than an overlay — the shape a lift needs — so the fix was the wrapper `searchDock`'s own note already assumed one screen over (*"the same composition, for the same reason"*), and nothing else moved. **The cost this named was closed rather than kept** (owner's call, same pass): the footer went on spending `insets.bottom` on a home indicator the keyboard was covering, so the button floated about 34 pt above the keys it had just been lifted onto. It was left alone at first for the right reason and the wrong conclusion — `searchDock` had the identical padding for the identical reason, so fixing one would have made two answers to one question. That is an argument for **one owner**, not for leaving it: `liftedBottomInset` (theme/styles.ts) now decides it and both call sites ask, with `useKeyboardShown` (hooks/) supplying the only thing either needs to know. It deliberately measures nothing — how far a control moves is `KeyboardAvoidingView`'s answer and a second opinion about it here would be the very duplication this consolidates.

  **`‹ Cancel` and the edge-swipe discarded a filled-in dive in silence.** That is *Delete dive*'s loss arriving through a quieter door — and on a boat, one thumb, the easier door to lean on — so `confirmDestructive` gained its second caller and its docblock stopped claiming one. Dirtiness is react-hook-form's own `isDirty` and has to be: §2.1's carry-over re-seeds the form through `values` after `useDives()` resolves, so a hand-rolled comparison would count the prefill as the diver's work and ask every dive after the first to be discarded having had nothing typed into it — a question asked so often it is answered without reading, which is worse than no question.

  **The gesture had to be disabled rather than guarded, and that is the half the plan could not have predicted.** `beforeRemove` covers every exit that goes through JS; iOS' edge-swipe is not one. The native stack pops before JS is consulted, so the first build asked *"Discard this dive?"* and left anyway, the question hanging over the list it had already returned to, with React Navigation's own error underneath — *"removed natively but didn't get removed from JS state… not fully supported in native-stack."* So the gesture is switched off for exactly as long as there is something to lose, which is that library's documented answer. It is not a lesser fix: the promise was that no exit discards a dive silently, and a gesture that does not fire discards nothing.

  **And "the picker closes by tapping the field again" was a rule about the time spinner wearing a platform's name.** It never reached §10 — it lived in `DateTimeField`'s own docblock, which is part of why it went unexamined for three milestones. `onValueChange` fires on every wheel movement, so closing on the first one would pull a spinner out from under a turning thumb — true of `mode="time"` and false of `mode="date"`, where one calendar tap is a complete choice. Written as *never close on iOS*, it cost a silently re-dated dive: the owner picked the 7th, reached for the field below, and hit the 17th, because the calendar was still standing where that field had been. The pick is now the dismissal for a calendar, and the spinner keeps the rule it was really about. **And opening a picker now puts the keyboard away**, which the first pass made necessary: with the form's root lifting, an open keyboard stopped covering the calendar and started compressing the scroll it opens inside, so a date reached from a focused number field was clipped at the third week. `DateTimeField` dismisses on the way in — it is the only thing that knows a picker is about to be shown, and the rule holds on any layout, since no field can see whether the screen above it lifts.

  **A row that had one screen's assumption baked into it** (M3n). `DiveRow` withheld a logged dive's date because *"its trip header already states the day"* — true of the Dives list and of nothing else, while search, the map's site sheet, and the site and centre pages all render that same row with no header above it. Searching a site dived twice returned two identical rows differing only by number and time of day, on the one screen where the date is the whole question. **The default is the fix**: a row states its own date unless a caller says something above it already has, so the next screen to reuse it cannot inherit the silence by omission. `DivesScreen` and `ReorderControls` are the two callers that say so.

  **Three smaller ones, each a sentence that was true and unhelpful.** iOS' autocorrect rewrote site names as they were typed — `Divoka Sarka` became `Divoká sarka`, an accent added and a capital taken — on the four fields §2.3 autocompletes, two of which publish into a **shared catalogue under the diver's name**; `FormField`'s docblock had argued the opposite and using it settled the question. *Location access* and *Account & sync* rendered under the heading of the section above them, so the location control read as a third fact about cylinder presets. And Stats told a signed-out diver *"None of your dives names one that knows its country yet"* — accurate, unactionable, and fired for the app's default state (§5) beside a map showing their dives in three countries; it now names the act that fills it. **Not** derived from the dive's own pin: that is a reverse-geocode per dive (`platform/geocode.ts` is a network call), which a stats screen cannot do on read and a boat cannot do at all. On the shelf.

  **And a Czech one the English original could not have** (owner's wording): *ponor* is the app's name and the common noun for a dive, so `Ponor může připnout ponor tam, kde jste` reads as *the dive can pin a dive*. The brand steps aside where it collides with its own subject. Both languages also stopped saying bare *Settings* / *Nastavení* for the **device's** settings on a screen already called that — `field.positionDenied` had been getting this right for a milestone.

  **Dive import was pulled forward out of v1.1** (owner's call, 2026-09-08, built 2026-09-09 as M3o). The shelf below has named it since the plan was written, and §10 ranked it third of the four things deferred past v1 — and the reason for moving it is the same one that unfroze the field set: using the app. A logbook that cannot read the computer the dives were recorded on asks the diver to retype what a file already holds, and every dive typed by hand is a dive with no depth profile, which §0.4 then correctly refuses to draw. **Suunto's own JSON export is the first format built**, and it is the only one — the four the shelf names are untouched and stay there, because the importer is a registry behind one interface, so each of them is a new file and one array entry rather than a redesign. That is what has to be true for this to be a scope change rather than a scope explosion, and §10 records the decision that buys it. **This is a deliberate addition to the milestone, not drift into it**: it is recorded here rather than folded into the "also landed" line above, because those were defects found by using the app and this is a feature the owner decided to build.

  **What it does not do is draw anything.** §0.4 forbids a curve for a dive with no real samples, so the row sparkline and the detail chart are still owed — and *owed* rather than skipped, because until something put a real series in the database neither could have been honestly built or tested, only mocked. Import is that something, and drawing the profile is the next thing it makes possible.

  **The profile came off the post-release shelf and was built** (owner's call, 2026-09-10, as M3p — closing the paragraph above). §0.4 had promised the sparkline and the chart *post-v1* since the plan was written; M3o moved them from deferred to **owed**, and this is the owner deciding that owed meant now. It is the same kind of scope change import was, made for the same reason — using the app — with one difference worth stating: **nothing here could have been designed a week earlier.** The 60 s window, the 1.5 m floor, the complete-window rule and the median-interval caption were each measured against a real dive's real series, and every one of them would otherwise have been a guess dressed as a specification. That is also why the spec was deliberately written second.

  **What that stretch landed:** `domain/profile.ts` (the pure geometry — colour runs, axes, downsampling, the scrub's index lookup) · `domain/consumption.ts` (the windowed RMV, on `derived.ts`'s own arithmetic) · `DepthProfile` and `ProfileSparkline` · `dives.import_device` and the detail's `Imported from` row · `react-native-svg`, this app's first drawing primitive (§10). The row sparkline reaches every screen that renders a dive row, not only the Dives list.

  **Two costs are named in §10 rather than fixed**: what the graphics guard gave up when its SVG arm stopped reporting SVGs on sight, and the fact that moving the sparkline's downsampling into the map builder does **not** free each dive's raw series the way its own justification claimed it would.

  **Choosing a site or centre became a page** (#19, 2026-11-15). The issue was five defects in the form's inline autocomplete, found logging dives on TestFlight; the owner asked for a redesign rather than a patch, and §2.3 and §0.6 now describe what was built — *Choose a site / Choose a centre*, *New dive site / New dive centre*, every name a site, and guests creating. **The scope call was made with the design.** *Nearby* came into v1 with it — the *Near me* pill and the centre picker's *Near [site]* — where §0.6 had recorded the canvas's *near you* group as unbuilt because nothing in v1 knew what was near. Three things the issue wanted went to issues of their own: **the map picker** (#30 — a *Map* pill, a mark tapped to pick it, the water tapped to create a site there, and *Choose on map* on the create page, built on #21's pointed map), **a centre's address** (#31 — no column today, so a schema change and a migration the owner applies), and **guests reading the community catalogue** (#28, which must narrow §5's adoption first).

  **Sites and centres got their full field set** (#31, 2026-11-17). It began as the centre's *Address* #19 left out and was widened, on the owner's call, to every field planned for either, so that the server change is applied once and the build installed once: §6 has the columns, §0.6 the create pages and the two places' own pages, §2.3 how other names are searched and checked for duplicates, §5 and §8 the licence, §10 each call. **Nothing of it reaches the project until the owner applies it**: `supabase/README.md`'s `alter table` lines, then file 6, then file 4, then the build. That SQL has run only on a throwaway Postgres 16 + PostGIS 3.4 (2026-11-17, a fresh project and the owner's upgrade path; `supabase/README.md` records what it showed and what it cannot) and is not yet applied to the project; the SQL ran twice on the throwaway Postgres, the second time after a site's `entry` became a set and gained `altitude_m`, the `alter column` included. What happens to a place after it is made stays in issues of its own: editing and suggestions (#54), a diver's own version (#55), the admin area (#56), and moderation (#57), which now blocks the release (§8).

  **Left, and every one of these needs the owner** — that is the milestone's actual shape now, not a coincidence:
  - **Sign in with Apple and Google** (#13) — **no longer in v1** (2026-11-19, below). It needs an Apple Developer account and a Google Cloud project. Neither needs Pro; both are additional providers on the existing session, so nothing built is redone.
  - **Sentry** (#12) — a DSN.
  - **The branded auth email** (#14) — an SMTP provider on `ponor.app`, which is the same change that lifts the free tier's few-per-hour limit. **Since #24 the limit is real**: every new diver now meets an offer to create an account, so #14 has to land before the store release.
  - **Store listings, TestFlight and Play internal testing.** TestFlight runs since 2026-11-14; the listings and Play are #15, whose App Store half is in v1 and whose Play half follows it.

  **Unproven rather than unbuilt, and each needs a second pair of hands:**
  - **M2's own done-when** (#16) — a site created offline reaching a second signed-in device. Every piece is tested and the seam between them is not, which is where this project's real defects have consistently been.
  - **`delete_account`'s grant** (#17). The function is written and the privilege was never demonstrated; §8's requirement rests on it. One throwaway account settles it.
  - **The mail links** (#1, 2026-11-14) — a confirmation or reset link signing the diver in (§5). Every test mocks the client, and the Supabase project's Redirect URLs must list `ponor://`.
  - **A multi-gas import, and this one cannot be settled from inside the project at all** (M3o). Every gas in a Suunto export becomes a tank, which is the natural reading of an array of mixtures against §6's array of cylinders — and Suunto lists gas *mixtures*, not cylinders, so one cylinder carrying a deco mix would very likely import as two. The owner does not dive multiple gases, so **no example file can be produced here**, and inventing a filter against data nobody has seen is what the importer refuses to do everywhere else. It ships as written and the damage is bounded: `configuration` is null on an imported tank anyway, and the diver deletes the spare. **`State` is the likely discriminator if it ever needs refining** — both reference files read `"Primary"`, so a deco mix presumably reads something else. Recorded as unverified rather than coded against; settled by one export from a diver who carries a stage.
  - **A dive saved without its profile is now completed by trying again** (M3o; settled 2026-11-18, #9). `createDive` and `putDiveSamples` are still two writes, not one transaction, so if the first succeeds and the second throws, the dive is still kept without its curve, not counted among the imported, and the file named as *Couldn't save: SuuntoDive.json. Try again.* Until #9 trying again could not work: §6's dedupe found the dive by `(import_source, import_id)` and skipped it, so the curve was lost for good. **Now the re-import writes the profile the dive is missing**, and nothing else about the dive, and counts it as imported. It also covers an app killed between the two writes. §10 records why this, not a transaction. **A dive deleted on another device takes its profile with it** when the deletion is pulled (§7.2), which closes the one case the completion could not see.

  **Owed design work:** the equipment row's missing carry-over marks (#7) were closed on 2026-11-19 as replaced by #58, which replaces the field and is in v1. The drawn icon set (#6) has been built (§0.6).

  **On the auth email** (owner's, M2): Supabase's built-in mailer sends fixed templates and is rate-limited to a few messages an hour project-wide — fine for one diver, not for a store release, and unbranded either way. **Configuring an SMTP provider is what unlocks the template editor**, and it is the same change that removes the rate limit, so the two arrive together. `ponor.app` is the domain to send from; the provider's free tier is well above what a logbook needs. Not M2's problem — the default mail works, it is only ugly — but it is a **release** requirement rather than a nicety, because the confirmation mail is the first thing a new diver ever sees from Ponor and §0 has opinions about everything else they see.
  **v1's scope was settled on 2026-11-19** (the owner's calls, in #22). The **v1** GitHub milestone holds every issue on it, and §10 records why. **The rule:** v1 takes everything that changes the shape of data already stored, plus the core logging flow; new kinds of data and screen-only changes ship in updates. Besides the owner's items above, it holds #11 (the project must not pause), #57 (moderation), #59 (caps on community text), #60 (the server functions' grants) and #23 (version numbers), and it brings in:
  - **Logging, and the shape of the data:** #39 (dives placed at their site, and the dive's own GPS point taken off), then #30 (picking a place on a map); #44 (cylinders, pressures and presets); #58 (my gear, replacing *Equipment*); #63 (what one dive included); #40 (planned dives); #53 (salinity follows the water body); #55 (a diver's own version of a place); #25 (a computer's profile added to a dive logged by hand); #46 (a short explanation of each field).
  - **The catalogue:** the OpenStreetMap import (#76, §5), and with it #28 (guests read the catalogue), #36 (the picker stays fast as it grows) and #54 (editing and suggesting corrections).
  - **Out of v1:** Sign in with Apple and Google (#13), and Android — Play's half of #15, #48 and #64 — which follows the App Store release.

  *Done when: strangers can install it from the App Store for free.* It read *from both stores* until 2026-11-19; Play follows.

**The v1 field set was unfrozen on 2026-09-01** (owner's call, after using the form to log real dives). It had been frozen since the plan was written, which was the right default and the wrong answer once the form existed: the fields that turned out to be missing — weather, a visibility scale, a weighting *judgement* — are ones nobody could have named before logging a dive. What changed is the field set, **not** the milestone: M1 is complete and its done-when is met; this is M1h, a correction pass, and it lands **before M2's sync protocol** so that no deployed client has ever seen the older shape. See §10 for each ruling.

**Web is pulled forward out of v1.1** (owner's call, during M1d) — but as a **testing target, not a supported platform**: no store listing, no parity promise, and no obligation on later features to work there. The reason it moves is not only that a browser is a faster way to actually use the app than a device build; it is that the blocker lives in the **data layer**, which M2's sync will be built on top of. Finding out at v1.1 that synchronous SQLite-over-a-worker cannot work on web would be expensive; finding out now is cheap. **It works.** A browser build logs, edits and lists real dives out of wa-sqlite in OPFS, and survives a page reload — verified in Chrome, not inferred. The full account is `.superpowers/sdd/web-spike-report.md`; what the data layer learned, and what M2's sync inherits from it:

- **`openDatabaseSync` cannot be the browser's entry point, and no timeout makes it one.** The earlier diagnosis here — a race between a spin-loop budget counted in iterations and a worker still compiling 621 KB of wasm — was wrong about the mechanism and right only about the remedy. A dedicated worker's script is fetched and started *by its parent's event loop*, so the spin loop blocks the very thread that would boot the worker it is waiting for: measured, the main thread span 134 million iterations over 20 seconds and the worker never answered. The main thread has to yield once. `src/db/client.web.ts` opens the database with `openDatabaseAsync` and gates the app on it; every synchronous call after that works, in ~12 ms.
- **Every synchronous result of 256 bytes or more came back truncated** — `patches/expo-sqlite+57.0.2.patch`, an upstream one-line bug in the bridge's length header. This is the finding worth having early: the sync bridge was not slow, it was *wrong*, and saving any dive at all failed on it.
- **A synchronous result over 1 MiB never returns at all. A second, unrelated ceiling sits underneath it: wa-sqlite's OPFS access-handle pool is **six files**, and it only grows through an `addCapacity()` call `expo-sqlite` never makes — so an origin that has accumulated stray databases simply cannot create another, and the app reports `cannot create file`. Deleting individual pool files makes it *worse*, since capacity is the file count. Ponor gets roughly five spare slots, shared with anything else on the same origin. M2 must know this before it considers a second database.** The bridge's result buffer is 1 MiB, the worker throws while writing past it, and the lock is never released — the app sees "Sync operation timeout", which is not what happened. At ~600 bytes a dive that is a ceiling around 1,700 dives in one `SELECT`, and M2's pull batches must stay under it or page.

Four native-only gaps in the browser, none of them the data layer, and three now closed. `@react-native-community/datetimepicker` ships no web build — `DateTimeField.web.tsx` uses the browser's own inputs, which keep §10's "an invalid date cannot be entered" for free. `Alert.alert` is an empty function in `react-native-web`, so *Delete dive* opened no dialog: `platform/confirmDestructive.ts` now owns the confirmation, native keeping the platform `Alert` unchanged and web using `window.confirm` — which honours §10's actual rule, that the destructive weight belongs to chrome **the app does not draw**, rather than working around it. `expo-font`'s config plugin is native-only, so the browser drew everything in its default serif; `theme/loadFonts.web.ts` loads the same six faces, and `app.config.ts`'s `.ttf` list is now derived from `tokens.js`'s font map instead of being a second copy of it. `expo-symbols` reads a `web` key the app never supplied, so the search and entry icons were absent; `components/symbolName.ts` derives it for both call sites. **And the fourth is now closed with a sharper answer than it was written with** (M2n): `react-native-maps` on web is not a blank space, it is a **crash** — its marker modules call `codegenNativeCommands` at module scope and `react-native-web` exports no such function, so importing the map at all takes the bundle down. A `DiveMap.web.tsx` sibling stands in, and the real web bundle was exported and searched to prove the native module is not in it. "Will not render there" would have been a comfortable and wrong thing to leave in the plan.

**What a pressable row may say about itself** (#5; owed from M3n, and the largest single thing using the app turned up). §0.6 spends the chevron on in-place disclosure and says so twice — *"the mark is never spent on navigation"* — and §0.1 spends every hue on depth. Between them a row that takes you somewhere, or opens a keyboard, or flips a state, has **no mark and no colour available**, and the app is consistent about that rather than careless. The cost is that a diver cannot tell, before pressing, which lines do anything.

**Measured by using it, not by reading it.** An empty number row reads `Max depth · · · m` — a label with a stray unit; an empty picker reads *Not set*; an empty text field reads nothing at all: three vocabularies for one state. `LOGGED` is a two-state toggle drawn as a static badge, sitting where iOS puts Done, so a press meant for *save* turns the dive into a plan. *Use my location* and *Save as preset* are actions right-aligned in the value slot, in the face a value wears. On the dive detail, **nothing** separates a row that navigates from a row you merely read: M3k left a height difference between them, and issue #3's uniform 48 rows took it away on the owner's call (§10) rather than keep a cue that read as uneven spacing. Meanwhile the app **does** own a control vocabulary — the bordered pill on *Complete dive* and *Reorder* — which reaches no row.

**What a replacement has to do**, and it is the same brief as the icon set above: separate *press me* from *read me* with no hue (§0.1) and without the chevron (§0.6), across a form, a detail page and a settings list at once. It is deliberately not a per-row patch — a chevron added to the site row on the dive detail would break the rule §0.6 states, and adding a fifth empty-state word would deepen the problem it is meant to fix. **Related, and probably one job:** the equipment Yes/No chip below, which is a switch drawn in the shape the same form uses for radio options — a white *No* reads as "No is not selected", because everywhere else on that screen a white chip means exactly that.

**A drawn icon set** (#6; owed from M1h) **is built.** §0.6 holds the hand and the table, and §10 the calls that changed a screen. Still on this shelf from it: the icons #63 will use (Night, Training, the altitude glyph), drawn on the canvas and not built until #63 is.

**A "Fields I use" screen**, if anyone ever asks for it (dropped from v1 in M1i). It would hide whole groups from the form. The reason it is here rather than in §3 is that the group memory plus the clear control appear to cover it entirely — an unfilled group carries nothing and stays collapsed, and a carried field can be cleared into that same state. **The signal that would bring it back is specific**: a diver who says a group will not stay shut *and* who has already tried clearing the field that keeps it open. Anything less is the setting being built for a problem the rule already solved.

**Owed from M1h (#7), and it is a design decision rather than a gap:** carry-over marks and clears every field it fills except **equipment**. That row is five Yes/No controls with no per-value trailing edge to hang a mark or a ring on, so every dive after the first inherits five accessories carrying no mark at all — under a header that says *"clear any of them"*. Naming it in a code comment is not enough, and inventing somewhere to put the mark is a decision about what that row *is*, not a fix. It waits for whoever next looks at the equipment row — which §10 already defers separately, since M1h split suit, thickness and accessories and left the accessories' own shape for later. **Closed 2026-11-19 as replaced by #58** (my gear, now in v1), which replaces the field this paragraph is about and carries its carry-over marks.

**Typed or pasted coordinates on the *New dive site* / *New dive centre* page** (owner's call on PR #35, 2026-11-15) — a wreck's published position, say, or one copied from a chart. In v1 a position copied from the dive's pin is changed with *Use current position*, or on the map once #30 brings *Choose on map* to the create pages. It would reverse M1i's *"no one will ever type it manually"* (§10) for the create pages only, where the position is a place's rather than the diver's: the dive form keeps no coordinate rows.

**What #31 left for later** (2026-11-17): a centre claiming its own page; the languages a centre speaks and its social links, which its website covers; the wreck lookup; the OpenStreetMap import, which has since moved into v1 (#76); grouping places by town or region; and filtering sites by *suitable for* against the diver's own certifications. Divers' reviews of a place, and the figures a diver's logs can compute about it, stay #43.

**After release — v1.1 and beyond, roughly in order:** the admin area · photos (compressed on-device, per-user caps) · **the import formats M3o did not build** — UDDF, Subsurface, Garmin FIT and the PADI migration, plus UDDF export; import itself moved into M3 and shipped reading Suunto's JSON · site pages with community aggregates · social last, deliberately: linked buddies and site comments bring App Store moderation duties (reporting, blocking), so they wait until the app has earned them.

**Migrating from the PADI app:** PADI's web logbook talks to a GraphQL API, and community tooling already exports it as JSON/CSV (see [PADI-Logbook-exporter](https://github.com/karnovnik/PADI-Logbook-exporter)) — summary fields only, since PADI stores no depth profiles and rarely a start time. Our importer maps that JSON; a GDPR portability request is the fallback. Real depth profiles come from dive-computer files (UDDF, Subsurface, FIT) and draw the profile chart on the dive detail.

## 10. Decision log

Key decisions and the alternatives they beat — don't relitigate without new information.

- **Supabase over a custom .NET API** (v1): $0 and no ops; plain Postgres underneath, so migrating to self-hosted .NET + Postgres later needs no redesign.
- **No trip entity:** carry-over prefill + auto-grouped list instead; a nullable `trip_id` can be retrofitted (even backfilled by date clustering) if users ask.
- **Tanks as a JSON array on the dive row,** not a child table: never queried independently; keeps sync whole-row.
- **Dive number computed** (chronology + `dives_before` offset), never stored: backfill renumbers for free; no per-dive override until real users ask.
- **Starting pressure is not prefilled** from the previous dive (explicit owner decision).
- **Offline-first with whole-row LWW sync:** acceptable because each private row has a single author.
- **Sign-in optional;** guest → account is just claiming rows (client UUIDs).
- **Email OTP over magic links;** EU region (Frankfurt); SI units stored, converted at display.
- **Photos, web, imports, social deferred** past v1 in that order; social last because UGC brings App Store moderation duties.
- **Named Ponor** (§0): the same word in Czech and English, pronounced identically, and free where it counts — `ponor.app` registered. Beat Slate, Bezel, Sextant, Halocline, Atoll, Manta and Deco, each of which failed an App Store or trademark check.
- **Colour encodes depth and nothing else;** controls stay monochrome. The scale follows the order in which water removes colour, so it carries meaning rather than decoration, and depth is always shown redundantly as a number.
- **Dark and light both ship from M0,** not M3: the token set has to exist before the first screen does, and retro-fitting a theme onto built screens is the expensive path.
- **No styling framework — `StyleSheet` built from the tokens** (revised in M0, replacing NativeWind in §4). NativeWind v5 is the only line supporting React Native 0.86 and it is a preview that does not work: Tailwind's `@theme` needs a PostCSS setup Expo does not run by default, and once wired up `react-native-css` fails to deserialize its own compiled output — the same error breaks Expo's own bundled `@expo/log-box` stylesheet. Verified on a simulator, not inferred. A `makeStyles(scheme)` helper over `tokens.js` costs little and keeps the single-source-of-truth property; revisit when NativeWind v5 is stable.
- **`tanks` JSON keys are camelCase** while every column is snake_case: a JSON interior has no mapping layer, so the casing the app writes is permanent, and changing it after users have rows means rewriting every blob and re-agreeing with Postgres in M2.
- **No CHECK constraints on `rating` or the 0–3 condition scales:** §1's "never block a save" argues against hard database rejections, and a CHECK would also reject a row synced from a future client with a widened range. SQLite CHECKs need a table rebuild to add, so this is recorded rather than left to be re-derived. **The same decision covers the TypeScript types**, which stay `number | null` rather than literal unions (`1|2|3|4|5`, `0|1|2|3`): an M2 sync delivering `rating: 6` from a future client is a runtime reality, and typing it as impossible would make the repository's row-to-domain cast a lie. Narrowing them would also break the schema↔domain drift assertion in `db/dives.ts`, since Drizzle's `integer()` infers `number | null` and cannot be narrowed from the schema side. Range handling belongs at the form boundary — but as a **warning or a correction, never a rejection**: §1's "never block a save" binds the form as hard as it binds the database, and a diver on a boat does not get to argue with a validator. A value outside the expected range is saved and can be flagged; it is not refused. **Half discharged at the end of M1d:** the fixed-option and boolean fields now keep and flag a value they do not recognise rather than refusing the save, which is what a dive synced from a newer client will deliver. **Discharged in M1h, and the delay paid for itself.** The four scales became chip rows, which is what made the gap *visible*: a stored `rating: 9` matches no chip, so the row would have read as **unset** — the value surviving in the database while the screen denied it. As text fields they had simply shown the 9, which is why nothing had forced the question. **The flag is a sibling of `UNKNOWN_OPTION_NOTE`, not the same sentence**, and the difference is who could have produced the value: every other flagged field can only receive a strange value from a newer client, so blaming one is fair. These four are the only fields in the app where **the diver could have typed it himself**, and telling him it came from a future version of Ponor would be false. So the note names the number, promises it is kept, and attributes it to nobody — *"9 is not one of these options. It is saved as it is — tap an option to replace it."* **What the note must be is stated here; how it is defended is a separate question, and the first attempt got it exactly backwards.** The intent was a test asserting the *absence* of attribution, so that rewording stayed free and re-blaming did not. What shipped banned two substrings and pinned one literal — so a differently-worded blame passed and an innocent rewording failed, the opposite of the rule in both directions. Recorded because the entry asserted the property as built before it was, which is the same drift this entry's own "Still owed" half was written about. **The property is held by review, not by the suite, and that is the settled answer rather than a deferral.** The second attempt bans a *grammar* of origin — past-tense authorship plus `from`/`by`, proved to have teeth by rejecting `UNKNOWN_OPTION_NOTE` itself — and a review defeated it in one line: *"9 belongs to another version of Ponor, not to you…"* is present-tense attribution using none of the banned words. **No predicate over the string can hold this**; every syntactic approximation has a counterexample shorter than itself, and the failure mode is a check that reads finished. The only genuinely enforceable shape is to stop writing the note as free prose and compose it from a closed clause set — rejected as more machinery than one sentence deserves, and because it collides with i18next: Czech clause order and agreement do not decompose the way English does, so the vocabulary that made it checkable in English would stop being one in translation. The defeat sentence is written into the test's own docblock so it is not re-found later as a bug, and that docblock now states what it catches and what it cannot. Recorded also because this entry described the flagging as built for the length of M1d and it never was — found by a review reading the plan against the code in both directions, which is the only way that drift surfaces.
- **Empty numeric form fields must reach the domain as `null` or `NaN`, never `0`.** `Number('')` is `0`, and `derived.ts` classifies `0` as *contradictory* for `sizeL`/`count`, which voids the whole dive's gas figure rather than skipping one cylinder. A bare `z.coerce.number()` calls `Number()` internally, so it silently blanks a dive's RMV the moment a cylinder field is left empty — no rejected form, no flagged field, just a quietly missing gas figure. Every Zod schema for these fields maps an empty string to `null` instead. This binds the first file M1d writes, long before anyone has reason to open `derived.ts`; the full reasoning lives in the `COERCION CONTRACT` block there.
- **One owner for the `YYYY-MM-DD` / `HH:MM` string forms** (`domain/datetime.ts`, added in M1a): the contract was previously asserted in three modules at three strictnesses and enforced in none, so the same stored value got three verdicts — `'7:30'` sorted after `'19:00'` in the list while `timeOut('7:30', 45)` returned null. The rule is now **lenient about spelling, strict about meaning**: a value naming exactly one real date or time is canonicalised however spelled, one naming none is refused, and nothing ambiguous is guessed. Per §1 the write boundary never rejects — it canonicalises what it can, maps a blank `time_in` to null, and stores the rest unchanged; the read side refuses to compute on a value it cannot read rather than mis-computing.
