- **A comparison that is sound between two values from one clock stops being sound when one side starts coming from another** (M2g, and this is the sharpest thing found in M2). M2d's pull rule — take the server row when its `updated_at` is later — was correct when written, because push did not exist and every timestamp in the comparison came from the same phone. The moment `push_changes` began restamping with the *server's* clock, the same line started letting the server's echo of a row outrank an edit made on the phone after the push went out, purely because phones run behind (§7.3's own premise). **One device losing an unsent edit to an older copy of itself, taking the dirty flag with it** — no second author, no conflict, no error. Fixed by requiring the local row to be clean, which is what M2d's docblock already said it wanted and could not express with a timestamp. Look for the shape elsewhere: **it is not a bug that was introduced, it is a bug that arrived when the other half of the system did.**
- **A guard must count the world, not read the report** (M2g). Sign-out pushes and then decides whether to wipe — and it decides by counting rows still flagged dirty in SQLite, **ignoring what the push said about itself**. A push that returns success while storing nothing is precisely the failure a wipe must survive, and there is a test that does exactly that. The general form is this project's most repeated lesson pointed at runtime rather than at tests: *the report of an action is not the action, and where the cost of being wrong is destruction, look at the world instead.*
- **The trigger list was written before the state it existed to protect** (M2h). §7.5 named four triggers from M0 and none of them fires when a session arrives — and `AppState` is already `active` at launch, so there is no foreground transition either. The four were not wrong; they were written when sign-out did not erase anything, so the case they had to cover did not exist yet. The moment §7.4 gained the wipe, "a diver signs back in and looks at their list" became a state where the correct behaviour was to pull and **nothing anywhere would.** Found by an implementer asking what actually fires rather than implementing the list as given. Same family as M2g's clock finding, and the family is worth naming: **a specification written for one half of a system keeps its wording and quietly loses its coverage when the other half lands.**
- **A retry ladder is not connectivity, and saying which you have is the point** (M2h). "Connectivity restored" needs `netinfo`, a native dependency and a rebuild — so what ships is a failed cycle retried on a doubling delay, 30 s to a five-minute ceiling. It covers the case that matters and it is **later than the real trigger and blind to the difference between a lost signal and a server refusing this device**. Recorded as a substitution rather than an implementation, because the failure mode of an unlabelled stand-in is that the next reader believes the spec.
- **A guard nobody could ever have caught failing is not worth adding** (M2h, and the implementer declined the work rather than banking it). The pending indicator draws nothing at zero, so "the read has not answered yet" and "nothing is owed" render identically — a `resolved` gate would be correct, invisible, and **untestable**, since no assertion could distinguish the two outputs. Declining is right, and the reasoning is worth keeping because the opposite instinct is strong: this project has spent four milestones adding guards, and the discipline is defended by knowing when one buys nothing.
- **The Postgres reset window is still open, and it will not announce its closing** (M2). §10 already records the SQLite migration history being collapsed to a single file because the app had never run on real hardware, and that window shutting the first time it did. The server has the same window and it is **not** shut: the owner's project holds only test dives, so the six tables can still be dropped and recreated from `supabase/migrations/` rather than migrated. What closes it is the first dive he would mind losing — a moment with no ceremony attached, unlike a first install. **Until then, a schema mistake costs a paste; afterwards it costs an `alter table` and a parity edit in the same commit.** Worth checking which side of that line the project is on before proposing any schema change, because the answer is a fact about the data rather than about the code, and nothing in the repository can see it.
- **An optimisation you did not ask for can switch off a mechanism you are relying on** (M2i, the owner's find on the first real sign-out). Five wipes said `db.delete(table)` and meant it; SQLite read the absent `WHERE` as permission to clear the pages without walking them, which skips the update hook, which is what `expo-sqlite`'s change listener is built on, which is what every live query on screen is built on. **Nothing was wrong at any layer** — the delete was correct, the listener was enabled, the hook was registered, the hook simply was never called. The fix is a condition true of every row (`1 = 1`, naming no column, so nothing can rename or null it away), and the cost is the page walk the optimisation existed to avoid. **The guarantee is asserted on the compiled opcodes**, not on the SQL string and not on the rows: a bare delete leaves the rows gone too, so "the table is empty" passes with and without the fix and is a safety net rather than the check. Proved by removing the `WHERE` at each of the five sites in turn and watching emptiness stay green every time. The general shape, and it is the third instance this milestone: **a guarantee that rests on a side effect rests on whatever the engine decides to skip.**
- **The two folds are not one function, and pretending otherwise would be worse than the gap** (M2j, settling what §10 has had queued since M1e). `zelezna` now finds `Železná` on both sides — NFD plus a strip of U+0300–U+036F on the device, `unaccent` on the server — and **they disagree**: `ø`, `ß`, `æ` and `ł` fold on the server and not on the phone. Four cases are pinned by name. The tempting fix, hand-copying `unaccent.rules` into JavaScript, is a second copy of a dictionary **Postgres itself refuses to call immutable** — §4.1's defining defect, adopted deliberately, in the one place where the two implementations genuinely cannot be one. What makes the divergence tolerable is §2.3's shape: the device answers first and the server supplements, so the diver sees the union rather than one side's blind spot. It becomes a real defect only if a client ever pre-folds a query before sending it, which would fold twice — stated in `supabase/README.md` where a client author would look.
- **A narrower fold was chosen over the obvious one, to avoid manufacturing the divergence** (M2j). Stripping `\p{Mn}` is the idiomatic JavaScript accent fold and it also removes the Devanagari virama and Arabic harakat, which `unaccent` leaves alone — so the tidy version would have *widened* the gap between the two sides while looking like the careful choice. Ranges beat categories when the other implementation is not yours to change.
- **A rough fix is not extra detail, it displaces better detail** (M2l). `use my location` asks for `High` (≈10 m) — a single deliberate fix rather than a tracking session, so the battery argument for `Balanced` barely applies — and **refuses anything rougher than 100 m**. The obvious reasoning is §0.4's (never draw a curve without real samples), and it is not the one that settles it: `formatCoordinates` prints five decimals and §6 has no accuracy column, so a 500 m fix saved is a permanent one-metre claim with nothing anywhere to contradict it. What makes refusing right rather than merely tidy is **§5**: a dive's own point *outranks* the community site's surveyed pin on the personal map. A rough fix therefore does not add precision to a site that had none — it **evicts** a better answer. The pin is stored whether or not a site is picked, because §6 keeps that column exactly for the diver who disagrees with the community's.
- **A rebuild I reported as verified had not happened** (M2l, my error, and the second time this session I have called log output a verified state). `npx expo run:ios` exited 0, the app launched, and I told the implementer the dev client was rebuilt. The installed binary was **three days old**, carried no `NSLocationWhenInUseUsageDescription` and no `ExpoLocation`, and the first thing the simulator showed was the whole dive form failing to load. **Launching proves the old build runs**; it says nothing about which build it is. §10 already records this lesson from M1h — *"log output is not verified state, `get_app_container` is"* — and recording it twice is the point: the check that would have caught it takes one command, and the reason it was skipped both times is that the happy path looked identical. (`ios/` is gitignored, so a re-prebuild was the fix. On this machine `pod install` needs `LANG=en_US.UTF-8` or CocoaPods dies inside Unicode normalisation.)
- **A latch that cannot fail is not defence in depth, it is a claim nobody checks** (M2l). A ref guarding double-presses on the location control survived deletion with every test still green, and the mechanism explains why: `setLocating(true)` runs synchronously in the press handler, so the second tap always meets a control that is already shut. Removed rather than kept "just in case", with a note naming why the save control's own `savingRef` is genuinely different — that one awaits a resolver before any state moves. Two other verification findings from the same pass, both worth carrying: `disabled` on a pressable is **announced** rather than enforced, and the testing library refuses a press to it while a device does not, so the assertion has to read the responder gate the prop actually closes; and a timeout test under fake timers **hangs** rather than fails when mutated, because the fake clock also fakes the runner's own timeout.
- **The map's marks are monochrome, and the coordinator was wrong about it** (M2n). I briefed the pin question as genuinely open and said I expected the answer to be *yes, a pin takes its depth band* — the one place §0.1's rule and a map's own nature agree. The implementer answered no, and the argument that settles it needs no measurement at all: **§3's unit on that screen is the site**, badged with a count, so an ordinary mark already stands for several dives at several depths, and §10 ruled on aggregates in M1l. Colouring only the marks that happen to hold one dive would make hue mean *how many dives are here*. The measured half — the scale is tuned as ink and inverts as a fill, a green pin reads as an island, a numbered blue disc is Apple's road shield, and a coloured fill has no ink left for "selected" — is corroboration rather than the reason. **Worth recording as a process note as much as a design one:** the brief asked for an answer rather than agreement, said so explicitly, and got one that reversed the coordinator's stated expectation. That is what asking properly is for.
- **"Has no web support" and "crashes the bundle" are different claims, and §9 had the comfortable one** (M2n). `react-native-maps` on web does not fail to render — its marker modules call `codegenNativeCommands` at module scope, `react-native-web` exports no such function, and importing the map takes the whole bundle down. The distinction matters because the plan's wording implied a screen that degrades, and what existed was a build that dies before any screen renders. A `.web.tsx` sibling stands in, and the fix was **verified by exporting the real web bundle and searching it** rather than by reasoning about the import graph — which is the same discipline as reading the app container instead of a command's exit code, applied to a bundler.
- **A trigger keyed on novelty would refuse the site you dive every week** (M2o; my brief's error, the twenty-sixth this session and the most useful). I wrote that the *add this site* offer should appear "when what the diver has typed matches nothing they have used before". Every dive logged before this feature existed carries a site name and a **null id** — so a home site logged forty times matches something used before, and would never once have been offered. The forty dives are not evidence against publishing it; **they are forty pieces of evidence that it has never been published.** The real gate is *does this name already name a row*, which the paired id answers directly. The general shape is worth carrying: **"has the diver seen this" and "does this exist" are different questions, and history is the wrong witness for the second.** (The offer this gated was retired by #19 — below — which lists the existing row above *Create* instead of deciding whether to offer one; the lesson outlives the gate.)
- **What a place is, versus what a day was, decides what a new site inherits** (M2o). The question looks like taste and is not: §2.2 already sorts the dive form's fields into the place and the day, and §2.1 then prefills a new dive *from* the site's own defaults. So the three fields that carry over are the three §2.2 calls properties of the place — `entry`, `salinity`, `water_body` — and seeding nothing would leave §2.1's prefill with nothing to draw on for every site this app creates. **`max_depth_m` is the instructive exclusion**: it looks like the same kind of fact and is not, because §6 defines it as the *site's* depth, so a 12 m dive on a 40 m wall would publish a wall as 12 m deep. And a **centre inherits nothing but its name**, because the pin on the form is where the diver got into the water — writing it to a dive centre files a dive site as the shop's address.
- **The offline duplicate check is deliberately weaker than the online one, and that is what keeps it honest** (M2p). The device could approximate `pg_trgm` in JavaScript and catch `Sharks Point` against `Shark Point`; it does not. **A second implementation of "what counts as a duplicate" would be free to drift from `name_match_floor()`**, invisibly, in the one place a diver cannot check it — §4.1's defining defect, in a form where the two answers disagree only occasionally and never say so. What it does instead is exact equality of the **folded whole name**, which costs a read it could already do and catches the duplicate §5 actually names: the same site typed with and without its accents. Substring matching was the tempting middle ground and is simply wrong — it makes `Kotelna` a duplicate of `Kotelna II`. **The general rule: where two implementations of one judgement cannot be kept identical, make the weaker one obviously weaker rather than approximately equal.**
- **A test whose fake answers proves nothing about a device with no server** (M2p, and it was predicted in the brief and found anyway). The offline path's guard could be deleted with the test still green, because the unconfigured cloud had no client at all and the call threw into the same `catch` the guard's absence would reach. The fix is the only shape that isolates the claim: a cloud that **carries a client while saying it is unconfigured**, so the discriminant is the only thing that can decide. A blanket `try` around the whole routine was making three further checks unfalsifiable the same way, and was narrowed to the one call that can throw. Predicting where the vacuous guard would be did not prevent it; **writing the test that could distinguish the cases is a separate act from knowing which cases there are.**
- **A suspicion is about a pair, so it is a row and not a column** (M2q, and it is the cleanest answer this project has found to a problem it has now met three times). §5 needs "this site might duplicate that one" to have three states — never suspected, open, and answered — and the reflex is a nullable column on `dive_sites`, which has two. M1i already paid for that mistake with the form-group memory, where *never decided* and *dismissed* collapsed into the same absent row. **The table makes the third state free**: absence is "never suspected", presence is the suspicion, and `on conflict … do nothing` is the entire implementation of "a rerun never resurrects a dismissal" — no second column, so nothing can disagree with anything. It also avoids two consequences a column would have had and nobody asked about: `pull_changes` renders whole rows, so a flag on `dive_sites` would broadcast every suspicion to every device; and writing to a separate table leaves `dive_sites.updated_at` untouched, so §7's last-write-wins has no server-side echo for the client to pull back. **Three problems solved by the shape rather than by three pieces of care.**
- **A decision's premise can expire before the decision is revisited** (M2q). M2c wrote into file 6 that the duplicate recheck must *not* run inside `push_changes`, and its stated reason was "there is nowhere to put a flag" — true then, and removed by the table above. The comment and the test asserting it both survived the change that invalidated them, and would have gone on forbidding the right design. Found because the implementer read the reason rather than obeying the rule. **The same shape as §10's `SectionList` entry** — a justification expiring while its conclusion stands — except here the conclusion expired too, and only the reason said so. It is now written the other way round: what keeps the recheck server-side is that **only the server can tell an arrival from a retry**, which no future table can remove.
- **A guard whose only job is to make another guard falsifiable must survive its own deletion** (M2r, and it is the sharpest thing said about testing in this project so far). The merge walk terminates on a visited set; an unreachable iteration bound sits behind it and raises. Deleting the **visited set** fails loudly — which is the point, because without the bound that same deletion would produce an **infinite loop**, and a hanging test reads as a slow suite rather than a bug. So the bound's mutation survives, deliberately: it defends nothing on its own and exists so that the real guard's absence is visible. Recorded because a surviving mutation is normally a finding, and this session has trained hard on treating it as one — **the exception is a guard that exists to convert a hang into a failure, and it should be named as such where it sits.**
- **The brief asked about sites and the rule was about "a site or center"** (M2r). §5 merges the two in one breath, M2a put `merged_into` on `dive_centers` for that reason, and a merged centre splits one trip into two through the trip key — as silently as a merged site loses its map grouping. The implementer built both. Worth recording as the counterpart to the errors this log collects: a brief that under-scopes is as wrong as one that misstates, and it is harder to notice because everything it *does* ask for gets done.
- **A promise can be half-built for five milestones and read as done** (found in M2r, about §2.1). *"Picking a site prefills entry, salinity and water body from the site's own defaults"* has been in the plan since M0. M2o built the half that **writes** those three onto a new site; **nothing has ever read them back**. So the rule works in the direction that fills the catalogue and not in the direction a diver would ever see. Nothing failed, because the writing half made the sentence look truer than it was — the same shape as "the same schema lives in both", the sticky-header justification, and "Fields I use": **a claim that is partly true is harder to catch than one that is false.**
- **A prefill is a function of what the dive holds now, not an accumulation of what it has held** (M2s). §2.1 says a site's defaults win over carry-over; the tempting reading is that each site *overwrites* and a `null` leaves the last site's answer standing. That version is path-dependent — switch from a lake to an ocean site with no salinity recorded and the dive keeps *fresh*, and if the diver then publishes a new site from that form, a **neighbouring site's defaults go up under their name**. The right shape recomputes from three inputs every time: what the diver typed, what this site says, what carried. **The general form: when a rule reads as "then apply X", check whether it means "and keep whatever the last X left" — those differ only in the sequences nobody tests.**
- **Three tiers, and the plan stated one** (M2s). §2.1 has said "site defaults win over carry-over" since M0 and never mentioned the diver, because nobody thinks to write down that a typed value belongs to the person who typed it. It is the tier that would have been most expensive to get wrong and the only one the spec was silent about. **A specification tends to record the comparisons someone argued about**, and the ones nobody argued about are exactly the ones an implementer has to invent — which is where a brief and an implementation quietly disagree.
- **A sweep that names the collection it iterates, and does not iterate it** (M3a). `symbolName.test.tsx` read as a census over `TAB_ROUTES` and was not one — so a fifth tab could have shipped with no Android name and no web name, drawing **nothing** in the browser, with the suite green and the file's own comment claiming that case was covered. §10 already records the shape this protects against (M1h: a raw symbol where a resolved name belongs is invisible to `tsc`, to lint and to a simulator that is always iOS), which is what makes the false census worse than no census — the earlier entry is the reason someone would trust it. **Ninth instance this session of a check that could not fail**, and the first to be found by a task adding a member to the very collection it claimed to sweep.
- **Rendering several states in one test can make its assertions vacuous** (M3a, found in the implementer's own first draft). Multiple renders inside a single `it` overlapped React's `act` scopes; the third came back empty and three assertions passed over nothing. Worth recording beside the fake-timer hang (M2l) and the testing library answering for the code (M2f): **this project's vacuous guards have come from the test harness at least as often as from the assertions**, and none of the three is visible in a diff — each needed the mutation that should have killed it to come back green.
- **A docblock claiming a test floors against the schema, over a list typed by hand** (M3b, and the tenth vacuous guard this session). `sync.test.ts` asserted that every flagged table is synced, above a comment saying *"floored against the schema rather than against a number typed here"* — and the body was `const flagged = [dives, gearPresets, diveSites, diveCenters]`. Adding a fifth table would have left it green for ever, which is exactly the defect its own sentence names. **Proved rather than argued**: the implementer restored the original body verbatim, removed `certifications` from `SYNCED_TABLES`, and watched it pass. The pattern to watch for is now precise enough to grep — **a comment that describes a derivation, above a literal.**
- **A rule can argue against the thing it looks like it argues for** (M3b). §10's store-and-flag ruling exists so a value from a newer client is kept rather than rejected, and it reads like a licence to close every vocabulary — flag the strays and move on. For `agency` it says the opposite: the flag's own sentence blames a build the diver does not have, and that sentence is **false for a field the diver typed themselves**. A closed list would tell a BSAC diver their card is unrecognised, in the app's own voice, about their own qualification. The general form is worth carrying because this log now has three instances of it: **a precedent is an argument, not a template, and the second reading is usually the one that matters.**
- **A sparkline normalised to its own range lies about a flat series** (M3d). Min-to-max scaling is the default in every charting library and it is wrong for this figure: 14.8 against 14.9 l/min fills the row with a cliff, directly beside a Trend row that correctly reads *steady*. Measured from zero, the same pair is two bars of nearly equal height, which is the truth. The general form is worth keeping because it is the opposite of the usual advice: **auto-scaling maximises the visible difference, and a figure whose point is "has this changed?" wants the difference shown at its real size.**
- **A guard's docblock claimed a capability the framework cannot give it** (M3d, the eleventh vacuous-check finding this session and the second in `unexpectedGraphics`). Its comment says the type-name sweep catches *"a component simply named for the thing it draws (Chart, Sparkline, Profile)"*. It cannot: the testing library walks **host** elements, so a component's type is always `View` by the time the sweep sees it. The svg and image halves work; that sentence never has. Found by probing rather than by reading, which is the only way this class is ever found — and it is worth noting that the guard's *other* half caught a real defect in the same task, which is exactly why the false half would have been believed.
- **A 48 dp box around a map annotation is not a tap target** (M3c, and it is the sharpest thing the simulator has taught this project). §0.5's floor was met the way it is met everywhere else — a transparent box around a small mark — and on a map it bought **nothing**: an annotation is hit-tested over the pixels it draws, so the 14 pt community dot could not be pressed at all, while the 26 pt badge beside it could. Measured by growing the dot until it answered. **Every test passed throughout**, because a mocked map renders a component and never asks what a finger would hit. The same pass found that a mark's position followed its size, so the marks had never been on their coordinates — under a comment saying they were. **Two defects in a screen that shipped a day earlier with 24 mutations all killed**: the mutation discipline defends what the code says, and neither of these was in the code's vocabulary.
- **Building both and looking beat arguing about either** (M3c). Sites and centres in one layer needs a mark to tell them apart, and §0.1 leaves only shape. The reasoning could have gone either way; what settled it was seeding both around Split and reading them — a square beside a circle is **the same mark drawn wrong**, two overlapping squares are one stacked card, and the layer's own count could not describe a mixed population (`Community · 3 sites` over one site and two centres). A **third mode** needs no new mark at all, because the layers never draw together. The general form, and this session now has three instances: **when a decision is about how something reads, the argument selects the candidates and only looking picks between them.**
- **Keeping the shape constant turned the failed experiment into the mechanism** (M3e). M3c measured that a square beside a circle reads as *the same mark drawn wrong* and that two overlapping squares read as one stacked card — findings that argued against ever mixing the kinds. The answer was not a better pair of shapes: it was **one disc with three interiors**, so overlap stops being ambiguous because two overlapping discs *are* a disc. The general form is worth carrying: **when two variants read as an error rather than a distinction, the fix is often to stop varying that property and vary a different one.**
- **An improvement was written, run, measured to do nothing, and deleted** (M3e, and it is the discipline this log most wants to keep). Lifting the *selected* map mark above its neighbours is obviously right and was implemented — and MKMapView does not re-sort a view it already holds, so it had **no effect**. It was removed rather than shipped, because a line that looks like it guarantees something and does not is worse than the absence it replaces: the next reader trusts it. What shipped instead is the z-order that does work — **the mark that says the most is drawn on top, so an overlap can only ever hide the one that says the least.**
- **A sentence can be false about the state that produced it** (M3e). The map's community layer said *"No community sites here yet… your next sync brings them down"* on a device holding thirty catalogue rows — all of them pinless, because §2.3 gives a new site the dive's pin and a site created without one has nothing to draw. The rows were there; the map had nothing to place. M3c had written a truthful sentence for the *centres* layer and the sites half never got one, which is how a screen ends up telling a diver to wait for data it already has.
- **Four rules were one rule over two tables, and what could not be shared was the same thing every time** (M3f). Sites and centres each needed "does this dive belong to that row", a directory, an RPC transport and a search debounce — and the part that resisted sharing in all four was **the pair of columns the screen asks about**, never the logic around them. So the shared rule takes the answer rather than the field names, and each table keeps a two-line projection. M3c's warning against merging them was about a *field selector at the call site*, which is exactly the part that stayed apart — **a caution against one shape is not a caution against the abstraction**, and reading which it was is the difference between honouring the earlier finding and being blocked by it.
- **Three of a task's own new tests could not have failed** (M3f, and the implementer found them itself). Two asserted `router.back()` on a way-out control — which every exit on that screen calls, so the assertion held whichever control was pressed — and one said "and no more" while asserting no such thing. Worth recording separately from the eleven guards found in *existing* code this session: **a test written minutes ago by someone holding the whole design in their head is not safer than an old one, and may be less safe** — the author knows what it means to check and can read that meaning into a line that does not carry it.
- **A test of a platform capability, run in the test runner, is a test of the test runner** (M3h, and the most valuable finding of the Czech work by a distance). Czech has four plural forms; the app shipped with **two**. Hermes on iOS has no `Intl.PluralRules`, so i18next silently falls back to `count === 1 ? 'one' : 'other'` — English's rule applied to Czech, with `_few` and `_many` **unreachable rather than merely unpicked**, because the library reported two categories and nothing raised. The map sheet read *"2 ponorů"* where Czech wants *"2 ponory"*.
  **Every gate was blind, and each for its own reason.** M3g's `Intl.PluralRules` test runs under Jest — which is Node, with full ICU — so it could only ever prove that *the test runner* knows Czech. Its simulator pass used counts of 1 and 11, which the two-form fallback gets right; **it takes a count of 2–4 to tell the rules apart** and nothing had looked at one. The polyfill installs CLDR's cardinal rule for the two shipped languages, and only where the runtime has none. The lesson generalises past i18n: **when a test asserts that a capability exists, ask which JavaScript engine answered — and if it is not the one that ships, the test has confirmed the wrong machine.**
- **A duplication estimated at twenty-five was forty** (M3h, discharging §4.1's "one deliberate exception, until i18next"). The field labels were counted once, by hand, when the exception was written — and the count missed the preset editor, the site page, and five screen-reader shapes retyped across eleven call sites. **A hand-counted duplication is a lower bound**, and the gap grows in exactly the places nobody thinks to look, which are the same places the duplication survived in.
- **The screen's mark for a computed value is a formula in a spreadsheet** (M3i). §0.6 has prefixed derived figures with `=` since M1b, for a good reason that does not survive leaving the app: a CSV cell beginning `=` is evaluated by Excel and Sheets. The rule was carried across as *the mark moves into the column name*, which is the only place a CSV can hold one. **A convention is a convention within a medium**, and this is the second time §0.6 has had to say what it becomes outside one — the first was `Alert.alert` being an empty function on web, where the answer was also to keep the rule and change its carrier.
- **Formula injection is a decision here, not a vulnerability** (M3i, and it deserves stating because the reflex answer is wrong). A note beginning `=`, `+`, `-` or `@` is executed by Excel, and the standard mitigation is to prefix an apostrophe. **This export does not**, because the data is the diver's own words going into the diver's own archive: mangling what they wrote is a certain harm, and the attack it prevents requires them to attack themselves. **The condition that would reverse it is precise**: the moment an export contains text *another person* wrote — a site's description, a suggestion's note, anything from the community catalogue — the calculus flips and the prefix becomes right. Recorded so the next person to widen the export knows they are also widening this.
- **A round-trip test is a writer checked against its own reader** (M3i). The obvious way to test an exporter is to parse its output and compare — and that test passes when writer and reader share a bug, which for escaping is the likely case rather than the unlucky one. The export is asserted against **literal expected text**, the BOM by code point rather than as an invisible character, and the result was additionally parsed by **Python's `csv`**, an RFC 4180 reader that has never seen this codebase. That last step is what made "the escaping works" a claim about the format rather than about our idea of it.
- **A safety gate applied one step too widely refuses the thing it was built to protect** (M3j). Sign-out will not erase a device that still owes the server rows — correct, and it was tempting to reuse verbatim for account deletion. It would have been **actively harmful**: after `delete_account` the rows belong to a `user_id` that no longer exists, so the push fails, the count is non-zero, and the gate refuses — **leaving a complete logbook on a phone whose account is gone**, which is precisely the state §7.4's wipe exists to prevent. The gate protects data a server might still want. Account deletion is the case where there is no server left to want it. **A guard is an argument about a specific danger, and reusing it is reusing the argument, not the code.**
- **The deletion has to survive its own refusal** (M3j). Start over tombstones the logbook, pushes, checks, and erases — and when the check fails, the **tombstones stay**. Dropping them would be the tidy-looking choice and would silently undo the deletion the diver asked for, on a screen already showing them an empty logbook. It also needed new words: the refusal sentence sign-out uses says *nothing was cleared*, which stops being true the moment the rows are already hidden. **A message inherited with a mechanism is not inherited with its truth conditions.**
- **A guest is offered nothing, and the argument is worth keeping** (M3j). The obvious kindness is a "delete everything" for a diver with no account, and every route to it fails: there is no account to act on; the act itself is the one §7.4 already rejected, at its worst, since the logbook exists nowhere else and has **no undo of any kind**; §7.4's own gate applied honestly would refuse every press, making it a dead control of the sort this project has now avoided four times; and there is no honest confirmation to write, because "this cannot be undone" is doing work no dialog should be asked to do alone. Guests delete dives one at a time, and can now export first.
- **A sweep that lists what it bans will miss what nobody thought to ban** (M3, found with the unit pair above). `DiveDetailScreen.test.tsx` claims to read *"every figure on the screen in the chosen system"* and enforced it by banning `m`, `bar`, `kg` and `°C` — so litres walked straight through, on the screen where gas used and RMV sit in the same cluster. It is the mirror image of the vacuous guards this log collects: those swept a set that could quietly shrink, and this one banned a list that could never grow. **The general form: a check written as a denylist is only as good as the imagination of whoever wrote it, and it reports success either way.** The honest fix is narrow — `l/min` is now banned by name, and a blanket `\d l` is impossible, because `12 l` is correct in both systems.
- **A sweep over the sheet cannot see what the app actually presses** (M3, closing the 48 dp guard's own gap). The tap-target check swept styles that *claimed* 48 and asked whether anything shrank them — so a style that stopped claiming it simply left the set, and deleting one `minHeight` took the floor off **four controls at once** with all 55 tests green. The fix reads the subject set **from the app's source**: every `<Pressable>` in a shipped `.tsx` and the sheet styles its `style` prop names — 57 sites across 21 files — after establishing that `Pressable` is the *only* touchable primitive in `src/`, so *being one* is the definition and the test decides nothing. That last step is what makes it different from the hand-lists this log keeps catching: **the enumeration is a fact about the app, not a judgement the test author renews.** It found `detailRow` immediately, which no reviewer had.
- **An explanation can be wrong while the observation that produced it is right** (M3, correcting M2s and my own brief that repeated it — the twenty-eighth brief error this session). M2s found three `shouldDirty` flags whose deletion changed no test and explained it as react-hook-form keeping any field that differs from its defaults. **That is not what the library does**: read out of its own source, `_reset` uses the seed diff only to choose which names to *examine*, and keeps what `dirtyFields` records. The real cause is a neighbour — this form never subscribes to `isDirty`, so a dirtying write whose value **equals** the seed makes the library re-derive the whole dirty set from the diff, sweeping in fields nothing flagged.
  **The consequence is the part worth carrying.** One flag's mutation survives because a *later* feature happens to mark the same field: M2s's own site-defaults fill writes three `null`s over three `null`s on the same tap, and that pristine write is what holds the paired id up. **A guarantee held by an accident of a feature that arrived afterwards is not held**, and the only reason anyone knows is that deleting *both* flags turns it red. A surviving mutation is a question, and this log has now recorded three different answers to it: an undefended guarantee, an unreachable branch, and — new — **a guarantee defended by something that was not there when the claim was written.**
- **The only witness was a docblock, which is how it nearly got filed as noise** (M3, the start-over clock). `tombstonePersonalRows` read the clock once per *table* rather than once per act, so one start over wrote three deletion stamps a millisecond apart under a comment promising one instant. It surfaced as a test failing **once** under full-suite load — the exact shape that gets dismissed as flakiness — and was proved real with a clock that advances on every reading before anything was changed. **Nothing downstream could observe it**: `deleted_at` has one reader and it is an `isNull`, `updated_at`'s two are per-row, and §7.1 has the server restamp it on push anyway. That argued *for* closing it rather than documenting it away — a claim nothing can check is a claim that will be relied on later by someone who reads it and cannot test it.
  The fix is worth copying: the stamp is taken **at the act** and passed down as a **required** argument, so a fourth personal table cannot join the sweep carrying a clock of its own without failing to compile. And the test runs under a `Date` whose every reading is later than the last — **not fake timers**, because a frozen clock makes the broken code *pass*, and §10 already records a fake-timer test that hung rather than failed when mutated.
- **A harness can pass a mutation test by luck** (M3, and the implementer caught it in its own first draft). Its test asserted "four writes, four distinct instants" — and the mutant that *never installed the clock double at all* survived, because the real clock happened to supply four distinct milliseconds. That is the same luck the original defect hid behind, reproduced inside the test written to catch it. Closed with a control no real clock can fake: every stamp must carry the double's own fixed second. **Third instance this session of a vacuous guard originating in the harness rather than the assertion** (after a fake-timer hang and overlapping `act` scopes) — enough that the harness now deserves the same suspicion as the code it exercises.
- **Half a pair survived a reseed and the other half did not** (M3, found by a task correcting comments rather than hunting bugs). Publishing a site under a *carried, never-typed* name and then letting a newer dive arrive wrote `{siteName: 'Silfra', siteId: 'new-site'}` — the form reading one site and the write meaning another, with `siteId` pointing at a row no screen could show. **No `shouldDirty` flag could have fixed it**, and the reason is the general lesson: react-hook-form keeps what it has *recorded*, and a write whose value equals the seed's is **pristine** — it records nothing. The id differed and was kept; the name matched and was quietly re-synced. So the protection belongs in the seed, holding the spelling each standing pair was made with, and it is the only thing surviving a reseed that guards a *value* rather than a mark.
  **A verdict in this log changed because a better test arrived.** The same paired-id flag was recorded a day earlier as unfalsifiable — and it is red now, because the new test has the diver answer the water rows *first*, which empties the site-defaults fill, which removes the pristine write that had been re-deriving the dirty set and masking it. That is the "guarantee held by something that was not there when the claim was written" entry above, seen from the other side: **the accident was in the test's setup, not only in the code, and a mutation is only as honest as the scenario it runs in.**
- **Three checks covered the row and none of them read the row** (M3k's mutation pass). Reversing a style array — `[detailRow, detailRowControl]` to `[detailRowControl, detailRow]` — left all 148 tests green and reintroduced the exact defect that pair exists to prevent: React Native merges left to right, so the reversal hands `alignItems` back to `flex-start` and puts a 21 pt line at the top of a 48 pt box with 27 pt of empty ink beneath, which reads as a row that failed to render. **Each check was looking somewhere else.** The app-wide `Pressable` sweep sees the *merged* `minHeight`, which survives because only one of the two styles names it. `styles.test.ts` reads the **sheet**, where both styles are correct in isolation. And the row's own test asserts array **membership**, which is order-blind by construction. Closed by resolving the styles on the **rendered** rows — `StyleSheet.flatten` — and asserting what the row actually ends up with.
  Two things make it worth recording rather than filing as a curiosity. **The mutation is not exotic**: alphabetising the array, or a tidy that puts the base style last, produces it — this is a defect a future cleanup introduces, not one anybody writes. And it is a new shape for this log: not a check that could not fail, but **a subject covered three times over by checks that each looked at a different representation of it.** Coverage counted by tests is not coverage of the thing.
- **A guard can be blind to its own subject while its neighbours cover for it** (M3k's mutation pass). Replacing an entire map-mark branch with a bare `<View />` left *"draws a community site in the ink a selection would move it to"* green — it compares styled descendants selected against unselected, and **two empty lists are equal**. Its one defence, `expect(mark).toBeDefined()`, guards the annotation and not the disc inside it. Two other tests in the same file did catch the erasure, so the suite was never blind — **the check was**, and a suite that catches something is not evidence that the test named for it does.
- **A tolerance is the defect, expressed as a number** (M3l). "Is the arriving kind already in view" needs a definition of *in view*, and the tempting answer is a generous margin — treat a mark just off the edge as visible, to avoid twitchy refits. That is **exactly the bug being fixed**, parameterised: a mark the diver cannot see counted as one they can. The opposite error is real too — a stingy test moves a camera the diver deliberately placed. So the region is taken **exactly**, edge inclusive, with no margin in either direction, and the choice is recorded because the next person to meet a jitter will reach for a margin first.
- **A standing prop outlives the thing that earned it** (M3l). The obvious plumbing for "move the camera" is a `moveTo` prop holding a region — and it is wrong in a way that has nothing to do with the linter that rejected it: a value that persists means a **rebuilt** map mounts holding a refit computed for a map that no longer exists. An imperative handle has no such state to go stale. The general form is worth carrying past this screen: **a command expressed as a value has a lifetime, and the bug is always what happens when the recipient is replaced and the value is not.**
- **An edge test that never touched the edge** (M3l, and the arithmetic is the point). A check meant to prove a mark exactly on the boundary counts as visible was computing its coordinate as `centre + span / 2` — and `43.5081 + 0.02 / 2 − 43.5081` is `0.00999999999999801`, so the mark sat just **inside** the edge and the test proved the ordinary case twice. Floating point turned a boundary test into a duplicate of its neighbour, silently, with a name that said otherwise. **Two of this session's guards-that-could-not-fail were arithmetic rather than logic**, which is a category worth checking for directly: recompute the fixture by hand and see whether it is where its name claims.
- **Suunto's JSON beats Suunto's own FIT, and the general argument points the other way** (M3o). FIT is the standard, the binary one, the one every other logbook reads — and choosing it here would have imported a **worse dive out of the same afternoon's file**. The owner exported one real dive as both, and both were decoded and compared, so this rests on two files agreeing rather than on anyone's memory of a binary layout: this device's FIT carries **no tank pressure at all**, its `record` messages holding timestamp, depth and temperature and its one `dive_gas` message holding oxygen percent. Five of the eight fields §6 gives a cylinder exist only in the JSON. **The cost is a figure this log has already written down**: `gasUsedLitres` is `Δbar × sizeL × count`, and FIT supplies neither the pressures nor the size — so no gas figure, no RMV, and §3's sparkline empty for the whole imported logbook. **FIT stays the right long-term target for a *generic* importer**, exactly as §9's shelf says. Suunto's own FIT export simply happens to be impoverished, and the lesson generalises past this device: **a format's reputation is not a property of the file in front of you**, and the only way to know what an export holds is to open it.
- **A file's format is decided by what is inside it, never by its name — and that is needed today, not later** (M3o). §9's PADI migration is JSON too, so the moment a second format lands two of them share `.json` and the extension has nothing left to say. Each importer therefore answers *do I recognise these bytes* for itself, and a registry picks the one that claims the file. **The consequence worth recording is what it takes off the screen: there is no format picker anywhere in the app.** The Settings row reads *Import dives*, the diver hands Ponor files, and the app works out what they are — so adding UDDF is a new file and one entry in an array, with **no UI change at all**, which is what makes §9's remaining four cheap rather than four more features. The caption naming which formats are understood is **generated from that registry** rather than typed, so it cannot drift from what is actually supported: it is the one sentence on that screen that could have quietly become a lie the day a format was added. **Recognising and parsing stay separate on purpose**, because they are two different sentences to a diver — *nothing here recognised this file* is a fixable mistake, where *this is a Suunto export and it could not be read* is not, and a single "failed" would collapse them.
- **A re-import skips a dive it has already imported, and never overwrites one** (M3o). The file is immutable — a dive that happened in February does not change — but **the diver's copy is not**: since importing they may have named the site, added a buddy, written notes, given it a rating. Re-running the same folder must not destroy the work Ponor exists to hold, and the same property is what makes a lost device recoverable, because re-import is then idempotent by construction rather than by care. **And the same rule settles a case the plan never asked about**: a dive the diver has since *deleted* stays deleted. Deleting is the diver's work too, so a re-import that quietly resurrected it would be the identical overwrite arriving from the other side — a tombstone is not a hole in the logbook waiting to be filled in. Worth recording because the built code was already right and was neither documented nor tested, which is precisely the state in which a later reader "corrects" it: `putDiveSamples` deliberately un-tombstones a *series* on write, and the asymmetry reads as an inconsistency until you notice that a skipped dive never reaches it. **One exception since #9 (below):** a live dive with no series row at all is given the profile its file holds. The dive itself is still never written, and a dive reaches `putDiveSamples` this way only when there is no series row, so nothing is un-tombstoned.
- **`series` was specified as Postgres `text`, and that was wrong** (M3o, my error, caught by tracing one value instead of reading the sentence). `dives.tanks` and `dives.equipment` are the two JSON-array columns this schema already had and **both are `jsonb`** — the answer was sitting in the file the spec was written against. `sync_row` renders a whole row through `to_jsonb` — the very property the M2b entry above keeps it for — and `to_jsonb` treats a `text` column as opaque: the array would have come back **as a quoted string**, the device's own wire mapping would have double-encoded it on the way in, and the next read's `Array.isArray` guard would have degraded it to `[]`. That is a dive's profile silently emptied the first time it was pushed — no error, nothing raised, and no reader anywhere that could have noticed. Nothing queries into the array on either side, so `jsonb` costs nothing and `text` bought nothing. It cost an edit rather than an `alter table` **only because the reset window recorded above is still open**, which is that entry being used for the purpose it was written for.
- **A rule naming an owner is a claim about that owner, and this one could not be met** (M3o). The importer's plan said `domain/datetime.ts` remains the only owner of the `YYYY-MM-DD` / `HH:MM` forms and that the importer "calls it and never builds a date string itself" — which reads as §4.1 applied correctly and **was never satisfiable as written**. Every *local* helper in that module reads a JS `Date` through the **runtime's** zone, and the entire difficulty with `2026-02-19T11:22:08.290+08:00` is that the diver's own wall clock is already in the string and must not be routed through any zone at all — the UTC trap §4.1 records three separate bugs from. So the importer shipped its own private parser, which is §4.1's defining defect arriving through a brief that had explicitly forbidden it. **The fix is a new export, not a call**: `localPartsOf` reads the local fields straight out of the string, hands the two substrings to the same validators a typed date and time already pass, and never constructs a `Date`. The general form, and it belongs to whoever writes the next brief rather than to whoever implements it: **naming an owner asserts a capability, and an unchecked assertion leaves an implementer choosing between writing the duplicate and reintroducing the bug the rule exists to prevent.**
- **A dive logged without a tank transmitter omits its pressures in two different shapes, and the obvious signal is not one of them** (M3o, verified against a second real export rather than reasoned about). In the header the gas entry **omits `StartPressure` and `EndPressure` as keys** — absent, not null — while `TankSize`, `TankFillPressure`, `Oxygen` and `Helium` are all still present and import exactly as before. In the samples, the cylinder's pressure is present and **explicitly null**. One fact, two encodings, two places, and a mapping that handles only one of them is wrong on the very file it looks right on. **And `TransmitterID` is not the discriminator**, which is the trap worth writing down: it is still there on the pod-less dive, so the natural check — *did this dive have a transmitter?* — concludes there are pressures and reads `undefined` as a number. The test is `StartPressure` / `EndPressure` themselves. **The result is correct and is stated here so that nobody repairs it**: such a dive imports with `startBar` and `endBar` null, so its gas figure and its RMV are null. It shows no RMV because no gas was measured, not because the import lost something.
- **A dense curve is a different object from a bar row, and `react-native-svg` earned its place** (M3p, amending the ruling §3 states beside the RMV sparkline). That rule, as it stood — *"this app has no drawing primitive, `react-native-svg` is deliberately absent, and a bar row is what the first-run legend and the rating dots are already made of"* — was argued over six legend bars, five rating dots and a row of RMV bars, every one of them a rectangle. A depth profile is not, and the difference is **countable rather than aesthetic**: the reference dive draws as roughly 420 rotated `View`s against ten to thirty `Path`s, and the row sparkline as about forty views **per row** on the Dives list — some 600 extra native views for fifteen visible rows, to draw fifteen small monochrome curves. **The rotated-`View` technique was built and looked at rather than reasoned about**: one absolutely-positioned `View` per segment, `width` the distance between points, rotated by `atan2(dy, dx)`, each extended by its own stroke width so the joints overlap. It works, it reads as a line at a glance, and it facets where the curve turns sharply — which is what the owner saw and named. **`RmvSparkline` is deliberately not converted**, because it genuinely is a bar row and the original reasoning still covers it word for word. The cost, stated: a native dependency (pinned exactly, `react-native-svg` 15.15.4, so the version matches the SDK) and one more dev-client rebuild. **The general form is worth keeping**: a rule that names a *primitive* is really a rule about the objects it was argued over, and the honest test when a new object arrives is not whether the rule still reads well but whether its argument still describes what is in front of you.
- **A guard that reported any SVG could not survive the app drawing one, and what replaced it is narrower** (M3p). `unexpectedGraphics`'s SVG arm reported an SVG element for *existing* — the right placeholder while nothing in this app could draw, and unkeepable the day two components did: kept as written it would have reported `DepthProfile` and `ProfileSparkline` on every screen that renders a dive, and been exempted away within the week, which is how a guard becomes a formality. It is a **paint** rule now: every colour an SVG node declares has to be one `depthScale[scheme]` or `themeFor(scheme)` produced; a drawing element that declares no paint at all is reported, because an unpainted shape inherits opaque black and an undeclared colour is therefore not evidence of a decision; and `RNSVGImage` is reported whatever it was painted with, since no legitimate node in this app is a raster. **The cost is real and is written down rather than left to be discovered: an SVG painted from the palette no longer costs a deliberate edit to this file.** An empty `<Svg>` reserved as space — precisely the placeholder §0.4 forbids — is a container with no paint to judge, and now passes where the old arm reported it. That half of §0.4 was never a whole-screen sweep's to enforce and is pinned instead by each component's own tests, which assert an empty render; what remains here is the half such a sweep can actually see, and it still bites — a chart in a hue nobody chose, a shape naming no paint, a group tinting its children, a coloured canvas, or a dark-palette stroke on a light render. **The recurring trade**: a guard broad enough to catch everything is the guard the first legitimate case retires, and a narrower rule that survives contact is worth more than a broad one that gets exempted.
- **180 s was chosen first, for a sound reason, and the reason had already been answered by another rule** (M3p). Raw 10-second readings on the reference dive are about 10 % non-positive — the transmitter reports pressure *rising* — and a negative RMV claims the diver produced gas, so a window long enough to average that away looked obviously right. It was wrong, and not because the observation was: those negatives are the **surface artefact**, and the 1.5 m floor, adopted for a completely separate reason (approaching the surface `ata` approaches 1, and a diver up there is often off the regulator), already removes them. So 180 s bought nothing the floor had not already bought, and it cost the curve its shape — flattening exactly the peaks the owner's own Suunto rendering shows. 60 s is what shipped: it reproduces that shape, has no negative readings, and its mean sits closest to the whole-dive figure `derived.ts` computes independently. **The general form has nothing to do with gas**: two rules adopted for separate reasons can turn out to overlap, and neither one's argument will mention the other — the only thing that showed it was measuring both windows with the floor *already applied*, rather than measuring each against the raw data that had motivated it.
- **A change that was right, sold on a benefit that was false** (M3p, found by reviewing the sentence rather than the diff). `sparklinePoints` was moved out of `ProfileSparkline` and into `toDiveSampleShapes` (db/diveSamples.ts) on the stated grounds that each dive's full series could then be collected the moment the map was built. It cannot: **`useLiveQuery` holds whatever it fetches in `useState`**, so the raw rows stay resident for as long as the screen that called the hook stays mounted, whatever any caller then does with them — and three of the six call sites that render a row with a sparkline (`DivesScreen`, `MapScreen`, `SearchScreen`) live under `(tabs)` and never unmount once visited, so for them that is the life of the app rather than of one visit. The downsampled points are retained **on top of** those rows, not instead of them, so the move left total retention marginally *higher*. What it genuinely buys is the thing it was not sold on: the downsampling runs once per query run instead of once per visible row per render — a scroll, or a sibling row's own state changing, included. **Named as a cost rather than fixed**: landing the real thing means not reading this table through `useLiveQuery` at all, and every other live read in the app is built on it. **The shape to watch for** is one this log has now found in its own prose as often as in its own code — *a correct change with a false justification*, where being correct is exactly what stops anyone re-reading the sentence.
- **A schema change made correctly under an open window still needs its own record, and this one was missing** (M3p). `dives.import_device` went into the base Postgres schema file in place rather than as a new migration — exactly right while the reset window above is still open — but nothing said so, and the omission is not academic: `sync_reject_unknown_keys` resolves its known-column list from `pg_catalog.pg_attribute` **at runtime**, so a Supabase project that has not been dropped and recreated from `supabase/migrations/` the way the entry above describes — or been hand-patched with `alter table public.dives add column import_device text` — rejects every `dives` push with `42703` from the moment a device running this code tries to sync. Survivable rather than destructive, exactly as that function's own comment promises (a sync failure never blocks logging, §7) — but survivable is not the same as harmless, and the repository has no way to see which side of the line any given deployment is actually on. The M3o entry above shows what the window is *for*; it does not show what is still owed once a change lands, which is what this entry is for instead.
- **The form's inputs were re-cut to the owner's design canvas** (2026-11-14; the owner: inputs "look worse and also behave worse than expected"). Measured on a phone against the canvas's own screens, four behaviours were wrong and are fixed — the label was dead to a tap, the return key closed the keyboard, a suggestion list could sit behind the save button, and a typed value could only be backspaced out (that fix, a ring in the focused field, was taken out again by issue #29 — next entry) — and the look moved to the canvas: the focused field is a card, and suggestions are rows with the diver's history in them (§0.6 has each). **Two places deliberately do not follow the canvas.** The carried mark stays grouped with its ring at the trailing edge, where M1h measured it into one column; the canvas draws it leading the value, which is where it was before it landed ragged. And a suggested site's deepest dive stays unbanded, for M1l's aggregate rule. **The row does not grow on focus** (48, where the canvas draws 52), because a focus treatment that moves the rows below it moves them under a thumb. The canvas also draws things v1 does not have — a *near you* group, filters, buddy linking — and none of those came with this: building any of them is a decision of its own, not a remainder of this one. (*Near you* has since been decided, with #19 — below; and the suggestion rows for sites and centres went with the inline list.)
- **The focused field's clear control came out again** (issue #29, 2026-11-15; the owner: "I don't like how the value jumps"). The input re-cut above gave a field being edited the carried treatment's ring, as the canvas draws it, and the value it sat beside is right-aligned — so focusing a row moved its value 60 pt out from under the finger that had just touched it, and leaving it moved it back. **Three ways to keep the ring were prototyped on the simulator and all three turned down**: as built; its 48 box reserved on every row, so nothing moves but every value is 60 pt narrower and the column no longer runs to the edge the canvas draws; and the slide animated (50 pt over ~180 ms), which smooths the jump without removing it. Selecting the whole value on focus, so typing replaces it, was offered as a fourth and not taken. What replaced it is nothing at all: values here are a depth, a time, a name, and holding backspace empties any of them. **The one value that does not fit that is a password**, which the diver cannot see — and **it is left to each platform's own secure field**. iOS's already empties on the first backspace after the diver comes back to it, and replaces it with the first letter they type; Android's takes a character per press, which is what an Android user expects of it. Ponor had a rule of its own first, and every cut of it was wrong in a way review found before merge: reading the Backspace key missed an Android hardware keyboard, which sends no key events, and left a Backspace that changed nothing armed against a fill that followed; reading the text instead emptied a new password on one typo; and on iOS any rule on top read the platform's own replacement (`hunter2` → `h`) as a backspace and dropped the letter, so retyping a password after a failed sign-in lost its first character. The last cut left iOS to the platform and copied iOS's rule on Android (the owner had asked to "match iOS exactly"), and **the owner then dropped it** ("accept platform specific behavior and move on"): it was never run on an Android device, it is not how Android's own field behaves, and it could not copy iOS fully anyway — from the text alone a letter typed over a value come back to cannot be told from a password manager's fill. A platform's own control is the default; Ponor adds a rule to one only for something the platform does not do. This is a departure from the canvas, and the only cost is the one the ring was added for: a wrong site typed by hand goes by holding backspace rather than one tap.
- **The account screen was re-cut for a keyboard, a password manager and a forgotten password** (2026-11-14; the owner: "the login/signup experience is not good"). Measured on the simulator: with the keyboard up the action and the mode toggle were both behind it (the dive form had been given a `KeyboardAvoidingView` in M3m; this screen had not), the return key closed the keyboard instead of walking address → password → sign in, the rows did not say what they held so iOS could offer neither a saved login nor a strong new password, the caret sat on a right-aligned `you@example.com` placeholder, and the two modes differed only in the button's verb under one *Account* heading. Each is fixed: the footer rides the keyboard, the return keys walk the form, the rows carry `username` / `password` / `newPassword`, the placeholder is gone, the heading names the mode (*Sign in* / *New account*), and sign-up says before the account is made that a confirmation mail follows. **One control now lives in the scrolling content, and that is M2f's rule applied rather than broken**: the toggle is about the button and stays with it in the footer; *Forgot password?* is about the password row and is drawn with the row, left-aligned on the column. §5 has the reset and the link.
- **The dive detail was re-cut to the owner's design canvas** (2026-11-14, issue #3: screens 03 and 14). The hero, the *Show on map* card, rows of one height with a hairline each, cards for the two things read as a whole, and the record's actions behind ⋯ — §0.6 has the screen as built, and §0.2 the sheet's three tokens. Checked on the simulator in both themes and both languages, for a dive with an imported profile and one without. **Where it deliberately does not follow the canvas:**
  - **The way out says *‹ Back*, typed**, where the canvas draws a chevron and *Dives*. M3k's rule — a way out names a destination only when the screen has one way in — and the detail has five. The chevron's drawing is every back control's, not this screen's.
  - **No chevron on the map card or on `Imported from`**, both of which the canvas gives one. §0.6 spends the chevron on in-place disclosure and never on navigation; the card's words say what it does, and `Imported from` has nowhere to go — there is no page for a source.
  - ***Show on map* opened the device's maps, not Ponor's Map tab** — **superseded by #21, the next entry**, and kept because the reason was true when written. The canvas draws the card and not its destination, and the Map tab could not be pointed at a place: it framed every mark it drew and had no way to be asked for one. Until #21 the device's maps did exactly what the words promise and added directions; `platform/mapsLink.ts` still builds that one URL per platform, now for the focused map's *Directions* and the card of a planned dive with no site.
  - **The depth rows keep their band colour and the row's 20 pt figure**, where the canvas draws *Max depth* and *Avg depth* in plain 15 pt ink. §0.1: a depth always carries its colour. This is a single dive's depth, not an aggregate, so M1l's exception does not reach it.
  - **Units stay in the value's ink** except on depths (`DepthValue` already splits them), where the canvas mutes every unit. Every formatter returns one string today; splitting them all is its own change, not a remainder of this one.
  - **The clusters stay §3's** — Date & time, Site & centre, Depth & duration, Conditions, Gas & cylinders, Equipment & people, Notes — where the canvas draws a sample dive in two groups (*Profile*, *Gas*). The canvas shows a dive's facts at the canvas's scale; the field set and its grouping are frozen in §3 and §6. Coordinates stay decimal rather than the canvas's degrees and minutes, and screen 14's computed average depth and minimum water temperature are not drawn: v1 stores the average, and has no minimum.
  - **The sheet holds *Delete dive* alone.** The canvas's other two actions are *Duplicate as new dive*, cut in M1e (§10 above), and *Export this dive · UDDF*, on §9's shelf; a sheet offering an act the app cannot do would be worse than a short one. A one-item sheet was weighed against Delete's old place — a muted label at the foot of a long scroll — on the device, and judged not worse: it names the dive, says what deleting costs, and is two deliberate presses from the record rather than one at the end of a reach. The hairline above the destructive action waits for there to be something above it.
  - *Edit* keeps the app's back-control face at 13 rather than the canvas's 13.5, so the two ends of the top bar are one size.

  **One place it follows the canvas costs something, so it is stated plainly: a row that navigates is 48, like every row.** Before this change the *Site* and *Centre* rows (when paired to a catalogue row) stood about 58 pt from the next line against about 26 for a fact, and that difference was the only thing on the screen saying which lines take you somewhere (#5's subject). The first cut kept a difference at 64 — the canvas's own height for a row that opens something, its search results — but on the simulator the taller rows read as uneven spacing rather than as a cue, and the owner took the canvas's one height. So until #5 lands, nothing on the dive detail says which rows open a page; #5 is where that is answered, by something a diver can see rather than a height.
- **A map pointed at one place, pushed over the page it was opened from** (2026-11-14, issue #21). *Show on map* opens Ponor's own map centred on the place with its mark selected, on a dive, a site's page and a centre's page; §3 has what the diver sees. The decisions, each of which could have gone the other way:
  - **Pushed, not the Map tab switched to — which is not what the issue's title said** (confirmed by the owner, 2026-11-15). It asked for *the Map tab* to take a place, and for Back to return to the dive, and both cannot hold: the dive detail lives in the root Stack over the tabs, so switching to a tab pops it and leaves Back nothing to return to. A pushed map comes back by swipe or `‹ Back` like every pushed screen, leaves the tab's own camera and filter where the diver left them, and is the seam §3's own note already asked for — *"the seam is a route, not a mode"* — which #19's *choose on map* wants too.
  - **The same screen, not a second one.** `MapScreen` takes a `focus`, and four things differ: where it opens, what is switched on and selected, a way back, and *Directions*. A second map screen would be the filter, the sheet, the refit and every empty and failing state written twice — §4.1's defining defect, by construction.
  - **A site is whichever mark stands there.** M3e takes the catalogue's dot off the map under the diver's own mark, so for a site they have dived the focus selects *their* mark — sheet, dives and the site's page in it — and for one they have not, the community dot. Both kinds are switched on for a site, because the layers are decided on the first render and which of the two it is is known only once the reads answer. **The camera centres on the mark it selects; *Directions* goes to the record's own position** (#27's two reviews). A place of the diver's own is drawn at one dive's pin, and neither the focused dive's own pin nor the catalogue's position for the site has to be that one — later dives drift by the GPS's own error, and an admin can move a row. Centring on the record's point could open with the selected mark at the edge or under its own sheet; routing to the mark's point would send the diver to a different pin from the one their card shows. So a focus carries both (`markAt` and `point`, `domain/mapFocus.ts`). A dived site needs no position of its own to be focused (Directions then goes to the diver's mark), and a dive with no pin of its own is never focusable, even where others at its site have pins — the map does not draw it (`drawsOnMap`) and its page shows no card.
  - **No map until the place has been looked for.** A `MapView` reads its region once (M3l), and the diver's own dives are on the map before the catalogue answers, so a map drawn as soon as it had marks would open on the dives and could never be moved to the site without a visible jump. It is a frame or two of a title over nothing, which §10 already prefers to a wrong answer.
  - **It opens clear of the sheet.** The place's sheet covers up to half the map, and a place centred under a five-dive sheet is the one thing on the map you cannot see; the camera sits a quarter of the span south, so the place lands in the middle of the half a sheet never reaches. A centre or an unvisited site opens no sheet and is centred exactly. The zoom is the one the map already gives a single mark, not a second constant. The camera never goes south of −90° (a place at the South Pole would otherwise make a region MapKit rejects). **Padding the map by the sheet's height was suggested on review and declined**: the shift was measured on a phone (the place lands about a quarter of the way down, above the tallest sheet), while `mapPadding` would move the Map tab's own camera every time a sheet opened or closed and change how every refit frames. A tablet in landscape is where the shift has least room, and that belongs to the tablet layout §9 keeps post-v1.
  - **The focus is where the map starts, not a selection it holds.** Once the diver presses a mark, closes the sheet or switches a layer, the selection is theirs — the same rule M2n set for a sheet never reopening on a mark the diver did not press. **A focus looked for and not found is let go of at once** (#27's review): the map opens as the Map tab does, and a place that a later sync brings in is one more mark — it is not selected, and its sheet does not pop up on its own over a camera that never moved.
  - **A link back to the page the map came from goes back** (#27's two reviews). The sheet and the marks link to dives, sites and centres, which are the pages a focused map is pushed over; pushing the same one again stacked site → map → site → map, and Back walked the repeats. The link whose page is the focus's own (same kind and id) pops instead — **but only when the page that pushed the map says the map sits over it** (`over=page` in the route, `MapFocus.overItsPage`). It cannot be inferred from the focus: a planned dive's map is focused on the plan's site but sits over the plan, and a link into a running app sits over whatever was showing, so going back from either landed on the wrong page and left the site's page unreachable from its own map. Every other link pushes as on the Map tab.
  - ***Directions* stays one tap away, as a pill leading the header's row** rather than in the sheet: only one of the three kinds of focus has a sheet, and a control that moved between the header and a card with what was focused would be two controls. It is about the place, not the filter, so switching *your dives* off leaves it.
  - **A planned dive opens its site, not itself** (the owner's call, 2026-11-15). §2.4 keeps a plan off the map, and drawing plans was weighed and declined: the diver's own marks count *logged* dives, so a plan at a new place would be a `1` over somewhere never dived, or a fourth kind of mark with no lever left to draw it by (§3, M3k). What a plan wants from a map is *where it is and how to get there*, and its site is already on the map — so a plan with a site id points the map at that site exactly as the site's page does (the community dot, or the diver's own mark and sheet if they have dived there), with *Directions* in the header. A plan the map cannot point at still opens the device's maps: no site id (a place typed by hand and never published has no mark anywhere), or a site this device does not hold or that has no position. **Whether the map can show it is asked of the map's own rule** (`focusTarget`, with the same logbook and the site's row — #27's two reviews), so the card and the map cannot disagree: a second rule here had required a catalogue position the map no longer needs for a dived site. The row is **read live, one row by id** (`db/useDiveSite.ts`), so the press decides with what the screen already holds — an awaited read at the press could open the map over a page the diver had already gone back from. (A double tap still pushes twice, as every link in the app does; the read only stopped widening that window.) A map pointed at a missing site opened on everything with nothing selected and no *Directions*, worse than the plan's own pin in the device's maps; a row not yet read, a failed read, or **a row that answers a different id** counts as missing — `useLiveQuery` keeps the previous id's rows until the new query returns, so just after a plan's site is edited the read still holds the old site (#27's third review). Unlike the Site row's link, which trusts the id because the page it opens can say "not found" and a map cannot. The plan's card shows the plan's own coordinates while the map centres on the site; in the ordinary case they are one point (§2.3 copies the pin into the site it creates), and where they differ the map is right to show the site it is pointed at. Whether the map draws a dive is asked of `groupDivesByPlace` (`drawsOnMap`), not restated as a status check.
  - **The browser keeps OpenStreetMap** (`platform/showOnMap.web.ts`), as the issue allowed: its map is a sentence (§9), and a sentence pointed at a place shows nothing.
  - **A chosen mark is lifted — bigger and floating — and every kind of mark gets it** (the owner's call, 2026-11-15). A focused community site is the solid disc, which M3k chose because a press never chooses a catalogue row: `action` is `fg`, so a mark at full ink has nothing to invert into, and a focus that showed nothing could not be found in a bay of sites. The canvas's rule needs no ink — `pin.selected = +4 px and a floating shadow` — so it reaches the solid disc exactly as it reaches the other two (`mapMarkLifted`, with the floating capsules' own shadow). **+8 rather than +4, measured on the simulator:** the canvas's pins are 14 and grow to 18, about 30%; Ponor's are 26 because that is the tap target (M3c), and +4 on 26 left a focused site beside two others reading as the same mark — in dark especially, where the black shadow vanishes into the night-mode sea and size is all that is left. +8 (34 pt) is the canvas's proportion and reads at a glance in both themes.
